Title: Following the Flow the Law Requires: Training Voice Agents to Transfer to Unseen Regulated Procedures in Debt Collection

  • Authors: Petmongkon Pornpichitsuwan, Krittin Pachtrachai, Touchapon Kraisingkorn
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18881
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18881
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.18881]] Following the Flow the Law Requires: Training Voice Agents to Transfer to Unseen Regulated Procedures in Debt Collection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18881)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Voice agents built on large language models (LLMs) now handle customer calls, but in regulated applications completing the task is not enough: the agent must follow a conversational flow set by law. In debt collection, it must confirm it is speaking to the debtor before mentioning the debt, identify itself and the creditor, state the amount, and avoid prohibited language. Every creditor turns these rules into its own procedure and revises it. We ask whether a small, self-hosted policy can be trained once and then follow regulated procedures it has never seen. One machine-readable specification per deployment, FlowSpec, generates the policy instruction, gates the tools, supplies the supervision and defines the tests; the agent speaks only approved catalogue sentences. We fine-tune a 9-billion-parameter model on 2,506 conversations from 297 randomized procedures and evaluate a Thai outbound-call agent on four unseen deployment procedures (three debt collection, one non-debt), one from a wholly withheld lineage. Documented checks, fixed by the procedure, rise from 67--71 percents to 93--100 percents, while grounded checks, fixed by the conversation, already pass at 97--100 percents before training; scenario pass rate rises from 15/43 to 40/43. An audit of the shared specification finds 11 defects, 3 of which its own metric did not expose.

Title: Physical Layer Anomaly Detection via Adaptive Gaussian Mixture Model

  • Authors: Mamady Delamou, Luan Chen, Arsenia Chorti
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18946
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18946
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.18946]] Physical Layer Anomaly Detection via Adaptive Gaussian Mixture Model(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18946)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Physical-layer spoofing attacks pose a critical threat to wireless localization systems by manipulating channel state information (CSI). Detecting such attacks becomes particularly challenging when the attacker and the legitimate node are spatially proximate, as their CSI characteristics become increasingly similar. To address this challenge in indoor scenarios, we propose an adaptive Gaussian mixture model (GMM)-based detection framework that learns legitimate CSI patterns and detects spatial inconsistency through statistical anomaly detection. Using a massive multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) experimental dataset with 64 uniform linear array (ULA) antenna and 5 mm spatial resolution, we evaluated our detector against CSI spoofing attacks with spatial displacements ranging from 50 mm to 450 mm. The adaptive GMM achieves 95-98% detection rate for displacements above 150 mm while maintaining 1% false alarm rate. We benchmark our approach against conventional GMM, local outlier factor (LOF), one-class support vector machine (SVM) and k-nearest neighbors (k-NN) classifiers, demonstrating overall superior detection performance of our proposed classifier.

Title: Limits of Statistical Assurance for Embodied and Agentic AI under Adaptive Adversaries

  • Authors: Inderjeet Singh
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18948
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18948
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.18948]] Limits of Statistical Assurance for Embodied and Agentic AI under Adaptive Adversaries(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18948)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Finite safety assessments cannot exclude an adversary that preserves unattacked behavior until certification and activates afterward. We characterize this limitation for embodied and tool-use AI. An adversary that matches the complete observation law until observable, irrevocable certification and retains intervention access afterward attains $v\mathbb{E}[(n-S_0)_+]$ expected post-certification events over $n$ trials, where $v$ is the active event probability and $S_0$ the unattacked certification time. The result covers adaptive and privately randomized evaluators. Switching at certification is exactly optimal for a fixed likelihood-ratio test under memoryless, event-increasing interventions without a binding intervention budget. A positive clean-certification requirement forces linear worst-case expected excess when intervention raises event rates over an authorized deployment horizon, even if certification is deferred to the deadline. In simulated RoCo collaboration, switching yields commanded-separation violations in 34 of 36 post-certification episodes. Preregistered randomized AgentDojo trials estimate 1.98 additional security violations per four-task continuation (95% interval [1.67, 2.29]). An adversarial language-model controller infers hidden certification from noisy evaluation receipts and times intervention accordingly. With permanent revocation, a mixture likelihood-ratio monitor bounds cumulative excess above a prespecified reference rate at square-root order, up to logarithmic factors, on every sample path. A single fixed alternative can permit linear expected excess under stationary attacks. Against a controller observing the monitor's rule and statistic, live mixture monitoring reduces violations from 12.00 to 3.81 per 24 assigned task opportunities. Revocation also reduces tasks executed.

Title: Evaluating Large Language Models for Multilingual Abstractive Summarization: A Comprehensive Study of English and Bengali

  • Authors: Khan Mahi Al Atahar, Nazifa Tasneem, Ajwad Abrar
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19003
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19003
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19003]] Evaluating Large Language Models for Multilingual Abstractive Summarization: A Comprehensive Study of English and Bengali(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19003)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Abstractive summarization remains challenging across languages, where linguistic quality and factual consistency may diverge, particularly in low-resource settings. We present a standardized zero-shot benchmark of eight closed-source and open-weight LLMs on size-matched English and Bengali XL-Sum test sets. Our evaluation combines lexical and semantic metrics with reference-free factuality verification and multidimensional LLM-as-a-Judge assessment, providing a comprehensive analysis of both surface-level quality and factual reliability in multilingual summarization. Results reveal substantial cross-lingual variation: GPT-5.6 Luna leads several English lexical metrics but does not maintain the same advantage in Bengali, where LLaMA 3.3 70B achieves the highest ROUGE and BLEU scores. Gemini 2.5 Flash obtains the highest observed Bengali factuality score. Furthermore, evaluation methods produce different model rankings, highlighting the limitations of relying on a single metric for multilingual summarization assessment. These findings demonstrate that strong English summarization performance does not necessarily transfer to Bengali and emphasize the need for comprehensive evaluation frameworks for low-resource languages.

Title: CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat

  • Authors: Bangya Liu, Jianhong Tu, Yueyang Pan, Hanhong Zhao, Zhe Ye, Hongwei Li, Saastha Vasan, Katherine Lambert, Junmin Zhu, Hao Wang, Jienan Chen, Yunze Xiao, Minglai Yang, Zihan Wang, Hairong Yin, Hao Wang, Zhiwen Fan, Tianneng Shi, Zhun Wang, Chenguang Wang, Dawn Song
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19005
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19005]] CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. Current benchmarks mostly evaluate agents in isolation against static targets. Real cyber operations, however, are dynamic and coupled: security postures constantly change as attackers and defenders adapt to each other in real time. Static benchmarks quickly saturate and miss an agent's ability to attack and defend simultaneously under live pressure. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. Beyond a benchmark, CyberArena serves as an adaptable, scalable experimental platform for future research on cybersecurity agents.

Title: Action Jets: Learning the Local Shape of the Action-conditioned Data Manifold

  • Authors: Anton Arapin, Chahyon Ku, Bernadette Bucher
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19007
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19007
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19007]] Action Jets: Learning the Local Shape of the Action-conditioned Data Manifold(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19007)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: World models are a powerful means of reasoning and planning, but many of these action-conditioned predictive models are high dimensional and computationally expensive. These challenges limit the number of action candidates that can be evaluated by a planner. We show that this cost is avoidable for planners over short horizons, which only require local information with respect to the current state. For a fixed state, the frames reachable by varying a low-dimensional action (a camera movement or an end-effector command) lie on a low-dimensional manifold. We estimate the local shape of that manifold directly via a network trained to estimate how each image region moves per unit of each action axis supervised by data collected from samples by the generator. A correction head fit to trajectory data estimates the shape of this manifold region up to a third-order Taylor polynomial. We call this network estimating the Taylor polynomial describing the manifold shape an Action Jet and compute its radius of validity in closed form. By traversing this approximation of the local manifold shape, we only have to perform inference once per state for any number of candidate actions. Scoring sixteen candidates from a single extraction is over 300x faster than with either source generator, and this gap widens with the number of candidates a planner evaluates. We measure against state-of-the-art action-conditioned generators, IRASim and WorldGym on BridgeData V2, a real 7-DoF manipulation benchmark, and FrameCrafter on DL3DV scenes, where we chart from the novel-view generators DepthSplat and LagerNVS. Because only the tangent comes from the generator and every other term from the data, the Action Jet also predicts held-out frames more accurately than IRASim, the generator that supplied its tangent, at short horizons and at over 100x less compute.

Title: PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization

  • Authors: Gor Simonyan, Tatevik Abrahamyan, Narek Abelyan, Tigran Fahradyan, Hrant Khachatrian
  • Subjects: cs.LG, q-bio.QM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19011
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19011]] PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011)
  • Keywords: fair, diffusion
  • Abstract: The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.

Title: Can LLMs Access their World Knowledge for Event Prediction?

  • Authors: Zijun Wu, Zhitian Zhang, Hossein Hajimirsadeghi, Nikita Dvornik, Alexander Pashevich
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19021
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19021
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19021]] Can LLMs Access their World Knowledge for Event Prediction?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19021)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Recent work has questioned whether large language models (LLMs) can be useful for temporal event prediction. Despite vast pretrained world knowledge, LLMs often underperform simpler specialized models. We ask whether this knowledge is truly inaccessible, or whether current methods fail to present event prediction in a form LLMs can use. We argue for the latter and show that LLMs become effective event forecasters when the interface is designed around two choices. The first grounds each event in a natural-language description, and the second separates event semantics from the prediction space to prevent semantic bias from label associations. To test this, we introduce DescribeEvent, a benchmark of ~150K events across seven datasets and six domains with event-level descriptions. We find that capable LLMs substantially benefit from descriptions and symbolic labels, outperforming description-free variants and non-LLM baselines, while weaker models gain little. These results suggest that accessing LLM world knowledge for event prediction is an interface problem, and that event descriptions offer a practical way to elicit the knowledge LLMs already encode.

Title: How Far Can Coding Agents Go in Visual Generation?

  • Authors: Hengguang Zhou, Yuanhao Ban, Jiaqi Feng, Xiaohuan Pei, Justin Cui, Cho-Jui Hsieh
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19025
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19025
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19025]] How Far Can Coding Agents Go in Visual Generation?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19025)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Modern image generation largely relies on specialized generative models that leave the process of image construction implicit in learned parameters. We explore an alternative: general-purpose coding agents that construct images through executable programs. Our study of zero-shot drawing on AgentDrawBench, a 200-prompt benchmark, reveals a distinctive capability profile. Agents excel at preserving structure and satisfying precise visual constraints, achieving competitive results in domains where compositional accuracy matters more than photorealistic appearance, while remaining limited in perceptual richness. Their visual expressiveness varies with the drawing interface, and a qualitative study on reconstruction shows how procedural skills extracted from human demonstrations support different artistic media. We then examine code as an external visual language of thought: a structured, executable representation that agents can construct and inspect. We test it in visual question answering task and find reconstructed visual code retains substantial information for question answering and, in some domains, supports higher accuracy than the original image. These abilities raise a further question: do agents draw on a shared internal representation of scene content beyond a particular drawing program? We explore an internal visual language-of-thought hypothesis, in which a shared, compositionally structured representation supports visual operations across drawing languages. Cross-harness drawings and images assembled from independent single-pixel queries exhibit related layouts and tones. Agents also maintain self-generated room arrangements across viewpoints after visible drawings are removed. These results demonstrate that the coding agent would maintain a consistent and coherent world inside its imagine even before the harness gets involved and the generation starts.

Title: How Many Ranks Does a Token Need? A Noise-Floor Squelch for Rank-One LoRA Experts

  • Authors: Mei Okonkwo, Owen Castellanos, Ingrid Solberg, Yusuf Demirel
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19033
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19033
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19033]] How Many Ranks Does a Token Need? A Noise-Floor Squelch for Rank-One LoRA Experts(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19033)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) applies every rank-one component of its update to every token, and mixture-of-experts variants replace this with a learned router that activates a fixed number of components. We argue that the number of components a token needs is a detection question with a classical answer. A token's responses to the rank-one components of a trained LoRA consist of a few large values on a Gaussian noise floor whose width matches that of an untrained adapter, and the floor carries cross-talk rather than adaptation. We introduce SQUELCH, a router-free mixture of rank-one experts that (i) soft-thresholds each token's responses at a multiple of its own robust noise level, so the active rank varies per token; (ii) chooses that multiple for each module by Stein's unbiased risk estimate, so no rank budget is specified; and (iii) replaces the resulting marginal keep-or-drop decisions by one explaining-away step on the value Gram matrix, which suppresses redundant co-active components and recruits complementary ones. SQUELCH adds no trainable parameters to LoRA. We prove that the squelch is within a logarithmic factor of the oracle input-dependent allocation, that the selected threshold is risk-consistent, and that explaining away is a descent step toward the joint sparse code whose error is controlled by the coherence of co-active components. On commonsense reasoning, knowledge, math, code, and GLUE with LLaMA-3.1-8B, Qwen3, and DeBERTaV3, SQUELCH improves over LoRA by 2.2--3.2 points and over the strongest mixture baseline by 0.8--1.2 points while activating 8.6 of 32 components per token on average.

Title: Combining Post-Quantum Cryptography and Quantum Key Distribution: Taxonomy, Constructions, Proofs, Standards, Deployments and Open Problems

  • Authors: Mona Alotaibi, Noora Fetais, Saif Al-Kuwari
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19039
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19039
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19039]] Combining Post-Quantum Cryptography and Quantum Key Distribution: Taxonomy, Constructions, Proofs, Standards, Deployments and Open Problems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19039)
  • Keywords: secure, security
  • Abstract: Post-quantum cryptography (PQC) and quantum key distribution (QKD) address the quantum threat from opposite directions: PQC replaces vulnerable public-key algorithms with schemes believed to resist quantum cryptanalysis, while QKD derives keys whose secrecy rests on physical assumptions rather than computational ones. Because each approach has a distinct failure mode, a growing body of work combines them so that a session key remains secure if either component holds. We first establish the deployed baseline, hybrid classical-plus-PQC key exchange, against which QKD-augmented designs must be judged. We then propose a taxonomy of hybrid PQC-QKD designs along eight dimensions (integration layer, combiner construction, authentication scope and source, QKD topology, trusted entities, key-delivery interface, classical component, and evidence type), and use it to compare published protocols, including Muckle and its successors, MACsec and TLS integrations, and IKEv2 mechanisms standardised in RFCs 8784, 9370, and 9867. We examine key-combiner constructions in light of the KEM-combiner literature and of NIST, ETSI, and ITU-T guidance, and map the security arguments for hybrid PQC-QKD protocols onto a common grid of security model, assumptions, permitted compromise, established guarantee, and limitations. We synthesise deployment evidence from metropolitan and national QKD networks, distinguishing the few testbeds that have run PQC in the key path from pure-QKD deployments, and summarise the standards and policy landscape, including the positions of national security agencies that recommend against relying on QKD alone. We close with open problems: authentication of the QKD classical channel, shared-infrastructure threat models, device certification versus formal proofs, formal verification of hybrid handshakes, and the lack of peer-reviewed deployment economics.

Title: Tracing Morphology, Semantics, and Divergence in Language Acquisition

  • Authors: Forough Shirin Abkenar, Haoshuo Fu, Liang Feng, Ning Yan, Masood S. Mortazavi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19041
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19041
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19041]] Tracing Morphology, Semantics, and Divergence in Language Acquisition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19041)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Understanding the dynamics of language acquisition in Large Language Models (LLMs) is crucial for uncovering how statistical representations evolve into structured linguistic capabilities. However, most existing studies focus primarily on fully trained models, leaving the developmental trajectory across intermediate training stages largely underexplored. In this work, we leverage intermediate checkpoints from open-source models (e.g., Pythia and OLMo) under teacher-forcing evaluation to systematically investigate language acquisition across training steps and model scales. We focus on four core characteristics: morphological structure acquisition, embedding drift, repetition dominance, and co-occurrence effects. Our findings reveal that models acquire morphological structures well before semantic embeddings stabilize. Furthermore, we show that training dataset size has a positive impact on divergence recognition, while co-occurrence during pre-training and repetition during inference have a negative impact on divergence recognition and associative link generation.

Title: Loss Curvature as a Sampling-Free Proxy for Memorization in Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Shashank Sule, Xinyue Yu, Boonam Shin, Yuxuan Liu, Abolfazl Hashemi, Hayden Schaeffer
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19042
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19042
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19042]] Loss Curvature as a Sampling-Free Proxy for Memorization in Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19042)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Understanding the mechanism behind why diffusion models memorize their training data remains an open question in generative modeling. In this paper, we examine this question from two perspectives: (a) the model-level perspective concerning the onset of memorization in a model during training, and (b) the sample-level perspective concerning the proclivity of an individual sample to be memorized by a given model. We show that the so-called input loss curvature, or the Frobenius norm of the Hessian of the diffusion model's training loss, is an effective metric for quantifying both perspectives on memorization. In particular, we empirically demonstrate that input curvatures averaged across training and validation samples can identify when a model is likely to start regurgitating its training data when tracked over training time. However, this metric is not sufficient for measuring an individual sample's likelihood of being memorized. To address this perspective, we introduce a counterfactual definition of memorization in diffusion models, extending the analogous notion from supervised learning proposed by [Feldman, 2019]. We prove that this counterfactual memorization is controlled by the input curvature of the leave-one-out model, thus establishing a direct theoretical link between a sample's local loss landscape and its generation likelihood. We validate our derived bounds through several empirical experiments and show that input-dependent curvature provides a proxy for memorization.

Title: Pre-Trained Language Models as Zero-Shot Tools for Social Science Research

  • Authors: Noah Dasanaike
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19048
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19048
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19048]] Pre-Trained Language Models as Zero-Shot Tools for Social Science Research(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19048)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Record linkage and demographic inference from names are routine demands in social science. Most approaches to these problems require labeled training data that are difficult or costly to acquire. I show that pre-trained language models perform both tasks accurately without requiring task-specific training. For record linkage, I introduce EnsembleLink, an algorithm that uses pre-trained embeddings alongside string-based scorers to retrieve a short list of candidates and then identify the likeliest matches. EnsembleLink matches or exceeds the performance of most prior approaches while generalizing across scripts and formats that those methods are unable to handle. For ethnicity inference from names, I show that large language models (LLMs) achieve up to 83.8% accuracy on U.S. voter files, compared to 78.0% for name-supplemented BISG. Open-weight models offer reproducible implementations that approach these models in accuracy. Moreover, LLMs are able to infer group membership for religious, caste-based, and other ethnic group categories outside of the United States, and reduce bias in downstream estimates of geographic racial gaps. Where pre-training encodes the world knowledge required by each task, task-specific labels add less information.

Title: Why Do VLMs Fail at Remote Physiological Measurement? Rethinking with Physiological Grounding and Numerical Alignment

  • Authors: Xiao Yang, Zekun Zhang, Yuxuan Fan, Duoxun Tang, Ziyang Liu, Dengbo He, Kaishun Wu, Jiyao Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19052
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19052
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19052]] Why Do VLMs Fail at Remote Physiological Measurement? Rethinking with Physiological Grounding and Numerical Alignment(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19052)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Video-based remote physiological measurement (RPM) has broad applications, while Vision-Language Models (VLMs) provide a promising opportunity to enable end-to-end physiological monitoring for future human-aware intelligent systems. However, existing RPM methods rely on task-specific models and cannot be seamlessly integrated with the multimodal understanding and interaction capabilities of VLMs. We revisit RPM with VLMs and provide empirical evidence for two major bottlenecks: generic visual semantic representations fail to preserve fine-grained physiological cues; while discrete token prediction in autoregressive generation is inherently misaligned with continuous numerical measurement. To address these challenges, we propose VITAL (Visual-physiological Token Alignment for Language Models), a visual-physiological token alignment framework for RPM and preserving general vision tasks. VITAL retains the original visual pathway for semantic understanding and introduces an independent physiological pathway to jointly model semantic and physiological information. Moreover, we propose a structured numerical alignment approach to improve the reliability of generative heart rate estimation by enhancing numerical relationship modeling. Extensive experiments show that VITAL improves cross-domain estimation performance on multiple rPPG datasets while preserving the general visual understanding capability of VLMs, demonstrating its effectiveness as a unified visual-physiological model. Code will be released.

Title: Deep Generative Models for Phylogenetic Inference with Complex Evolutionary Processes

  • Authors: Ethan Baron, Alan N. Amin, Andrew Gordon Wilson
  • Subjects: cs.LG, q-bio.PE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19053
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19053
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19053]] Deep Generative Models for Phylogenetic Inference with Complex Evolutionary Processes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19053)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Inference of phylogenetic trees from sequences plays a central role in understanding evolutionary relationships, with applications ranging from tracking pathogen spread to reconstructing the history of life. Conventionally, practitioners obtain posteriors over trees using likelihood-based methods like MCMC. However, likelihoods are only available for simple evolutionary models with restrictive assumptions. For realistic evolutionary models, inference using conventional methods is prohibitively expensive, inaccurate, or even impossible. In practice, practitioners settle for using as flexible a conventional method as possible, even though they expect it is misspecified. In principle, we could use simulation-based inference: simulate tree and sequence data from an evolutionary model and train a neural network to predict the posterior over trees given sequences. However it is a mathematical challenge to represent and train complex posterior distributions over trees. Here we present a flexible family of conditional generative models over trees. Our models iteratively build trees using three natural paradigms -- top-down, middle-out, or bottom-up -- and are trained using a discrete diffusion framework. For real and synthetic datasets of up to $65$ taxa and $>10^5$ tokens, our models recover MCMC posteriors much more accurately than the previous state-of-the-art simulation-based method Phyloformer 2. Finally, we compare to IQ-Tree, a popular flexible-but-misspecified conventional method. Our models match its tree estimates on simple data, but on more realistic data, our method is reliably more accurate.

Title: How Inner Optimizers Shape Replica Scaling

  • Authors: Eugene Belilovsky, Stefan Horoi, Edouard Oyallon, Benjamin Thérien
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19064
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19064
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19064]] How Inner Optimizers Shape Replica Scaling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19064)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) across geographically distributed and poorly interconnected compute pools or data centers has become a topic of significant interest. This has led to increased study of local optimization methods such as DiLoco for LLMs, especially those that use local (or inner) adaptive or preconditioned optimizers, which are critical for high-performance LLM training. However, a central challenge remains: it is well known that increasing the number of model replicas (replica scaling) while fixing the total training budget leads to monotonically decreasing performance. Existing work has empirically demonstrated that the choice of inner optimizer (Muon v.s. AdamW) can significantly improve this trend, but offers only limited explanation of the performance difference. In this work, we consider a range of inner-optimizers and identify two important factors that impact replica scaling: (1) the covariance structure of the pseudo-gradients and (2) the impact of batch size on the pseudogradient's noise scale. Based on these insights, we theoretically and empirically demonstrate that whitening-based pre-conditioning is a powerful inner-optimizer desideratum. Our empirical evaluation extends the results to reveal that whitening-based inner-optimizers achieve strong replica scaling (for 8-96 replicas), with performance relative to DP improving as parameters and data are scaled.

Title: MonoMIP: Multi-Height Inverse Perspective Mapping for Monocular 3D Object Detection

  • Authors: Emir Ulurak, Furkan Kıraç
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Monocular 3D object detection methods increasingly rely on depth-guided transformers, but they operate entirely in the perspective image plane where spatial relationships are distorted by foreshortening. Title: Backpropagating Verifier Rewards Through Continuous Flow Language Models

  • Authors: Xu Han, Jiajing Hu, Li-Ping Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19080
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19080
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19080]] Backpropagating Verifier Rewards Through Continuous Flow Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19080)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has substantially improved the reasoning of autoregressive and discrete-diffusion language models. Language models that generate continuous token embeddings are emerging as a strong alternative, but RL post-training for them remains understudied. We propose \method{}, a post-training framework for continuous flow language models. Such a model denoises token embeddings over multiple steps and decodes them into discrete tokens only at the last step. To obtain pathwise gradients, which backpropagate through continuous actions and have much lower variance than REINFORCE, we introduce a critic that estimates the value of intermediate token embeddings, bypassing the non-differentiable decoding step. The critic must also keep track of the value function as the policy changes; to this end, we design a critic that predicts a value from every token representation and improves policy optimization. Across four reasoning tasks, \method{} reliably improves a continuous flow language model through RL post-training and outperforms baseline methods of the same type.

Title: Beyond Aggregate Accuracy: Item-Level Transitions in Prompt Optimization

  • Authors: Chinmoy Mitra, Tarek Mahmud
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19084
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19084
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19084]] Beyond Aggregate Accuracy: Item-Level Transitions in Prompt Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19084)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Automatic prompt optimization (APO) uses feedback from model responses to improve prompts automatically. However, its effectiveness is usually measured through overall scores, which do not reveal which questions become correct, which previously correct answers become wrong, or whether improvements extend to unseen questions. We introduce Reflective Feedback-Driven Prompt Optimization (RFDPO), a tool that tracks recoveries (incorrect to correct), regressions (correct to incorrect), and churn (all changed outcomes). RFDPO uses this feedback to guide subsequent revisions and records prompt changes throughout optimization. We evaluate RFDPO across five benchmarks covering law, knowledge, instruction following, mathematics, and privacy, using GPT-5 as the optimizer and GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1 Mini as solvers. Our results show that overall accuracy can hide substantial changes: in five of nine configurations, churn was at least five times the absolute net change, and a single-item net change concealed up to 17 changed outcomes. Average validation gains were 7.9 percentage points, compared with 5.2 points on held-out tests. In a controlled ablation on LegalBench-Hearsay, item-level feedback reduced churn by 40% ($p=0.024$) while maintaining similar net gains. These findings motivate reporting recovery, regression, churn, and performance on unseen data alongside overall accuracy.

Title: Contextual Credit Policy Optimization

  • Authors: Huiwen Han, Ziyao Zeng, Zihao Zhu, Sisong Bei, Hao Wang, Hongyu Xu, Yan Han, Degui Zhi, Zhiwen Fan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19103
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19103
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19103]] Contextual Credit Policy Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19103)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning enables large language model agents to learn from sparse outcome rewards in long-horizon tasks. Recent methods derive step-level credit by comparing observation-matched experiences across trajectories. However, identical observations can occur in different contexts, and matched peers are not equally informative for a given decision. Moreover, favorable terminal outcomes can obscure redundant actions or detours within successful trajectories. To address these two challenges, we propose Contextual Credit Policy Optimization (CCPO). For the first, CCPO weights observation-matched peers from other trajectories by contextual similarity, computed from frozen LLM representations and accumulated trajectory statistics, allowing peers from similar contexts to contribute more to a step's baseline. For the second, CCPO pairs historical credit, which compares the realized return against the weighted peer baseline, with future credit, which measures the change in contextual outcome value over a short realized continuation. A step is thus credited for the progress it makes, not only for its trajectory's eventual outcome. Both credits are computed in hindsight from rollouts already collected for training, enabling CCPO to address these challenges without an auxiliary value critic. Across two Qwen2.5-Instruct backbones (1.5B and 7B), CCPO achieves up to 97.4% success on ALFWorld and 79.4% on WebShop and outperforms GRPO by up to 21.6 and 18.5 points. Ablations show that contextual weighting outperforms uniform weighting, and that historical and future credit serve as complementary learning signals.

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Generating novel molecules with desired properties is a central challenge in drug discovery. We introduce PairIT, a flexible training framework for autoregressive transformers over SMILES sequences that enables seeded generation by learning from pairs of structurally similar molecules. By leveraging matched pairs, the model learns to generate local, chemically meaningful edits that improve a property of the seed molecule. Surprisingly, we observe diminishing returns to the scale of the pretraining dataset. We demonstrate empirically on MoleculeNet and Therapeutics Data Commons datasets that, with appropriate paired-data curation, a standard transformer model of modest size (68M parameters) can achieve robust seeded molecular optimization in low-data regimes.

  • Subjects: cs.CL

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Rare but severe failures of large language models can become practically significant at deployment scale. Estimating their probabilities with direct Monte Carlo sampling is highly inefficient, while proposal-based methods typically require auxiliary learning of an additional proposal distribution. We introduce \textbf{Weighted Ensemble (WE)}, a training-free framework for rare-event estimation in language models. WE samples exclusively from the original language model and improves upon direct Monte Carlo by periodically reallocating the sampling budget toward prefixes that appear more likely to reach the target rare event. We provide a theoretical analysis of the proposed algorithm.   • Abstract: Transformers are efficient to pre-train at scale because attention permits parallel computation across sequence positions, but long-context inference requires increasingly large KV caches. Further, the current paradigm of extending long context tasks creates competing notions of retaining information precisely and keeping long-context inference efficient. Title: Decoupled Drift-Diffusion World Models for Consistent Future Frame Prediction

  • Authors: Anton Arapin, Aditya Singh, Yixin Wang, Jun Gao, Bernadette Bucher
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19212
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19212
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19212]] Decoupled Drift-Diffusion World Models for Consistent Future Frame Prediction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19212)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: World models of visual information predict future image frames conditioned on given action sequences. This predicted scene evolution is driven by deterministic changes such as controlled motion on a robot platform, as well as stochastic changes such as rendering of manipulated objects or previously occluded regions. Our key insight is that the spatial location of these stochastic changes is itself learnable. We leverage this insight by jointly regressing both the deterministic next-state prediction and the spatial uncertainty map, then using the uncertainty map to guide a conditional diffusion process. We propose Decoupled Drift-Diffusion World Model (D3WM), which uses a transformer trained with a Gaussian negative-log-likelihood objective to produce both the deterministic next-state prediction and a spatial map of heteroscedastic aleatoric uncertainty. The learned uncertainty map then provides a structured spatial gate for a conditional diffusion model: deterministic regions are anchored to the predicted state, while uncertain regions, such as disocclusions or dynamic textures, are sampled by the diffusion process. We frame this decomposition under a variance-gated stochastic differential equation that separates deterministic drift from stochastic diffusion. D3WM shows improved results over competitive baselines in two distinct types of action spaces: robotic tabletop manipulation and camera motion.

Title: Narrowing the Collaboration Gap, Probably

  • Authors: Mirah Shi, Marcel Hussing, Ira Globus-Harris, Natalie Collina, Aaron Roth, Surbhi Goel
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19215
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19215
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19215]] Narrowing the Collaboration Gap, Probably(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19215)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as teams of agents that hold different private information about a shared task. How should such agents concisely communicate in a way that promotes aggregation of decision-relevant information? Communicating only proposed actions can discard decision-relevant uncertainty: two agents may prefer the same action for different reasons. We study an alternative interface in which agents communicate calibrated beliefs over actions. We first analyze a simple synthetic setting that captures the structure of multi-round reasoning under partial information. In this setting, we show theoretically and empirically that communicating unbiased probabilities can be strictly more powerful than communicating actions. We then test the same principle in a collaborative maze-solving task with trained transformers and pretrained LLM agents. Our results demonstrate that probability communication outperforms action communication when the exchanged predictions preserve calibrated uncertainty. When communicated probabilities are biased, we show that the benefits of probability communication can disappear. We give a post-hoc conversational calibration intervention that consistently improves decision-making by correcting these biases both in a toy transformer setting and when pretrained LLMs use the corrections in context. These results suggest that probabilistic communication is useful for multi-agent collaboration not merely because it transmits more information, but because calibrated uncertainty gives other agents a reliable object to update on.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19232
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19232
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19232]] Spectral-MoE: Heterogeneous Per-Layer Expert Utilization in Dense-to-MoE Conversion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19232)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models impose substantial memory demands and arithmetic costs during inference. Dense-to-mixture-of-experts (MoE) conversion is a model optimization technique that aims to reduce these arithmetic costs. Dense-to-MoE conversion partitions pretrained feed-forward network (FFN) neurons into experts and selects a subset for each token. Leading methods fall into two categories: analytic methods produce a converted model quickly using heuristics, while learning methods produce a model much more slowly by optimizing via gradients. Both approaches, however, fix the same active expert count, or "utilization," at every layer. We introduce Spectral-MoE, which learns to allocate a utilization budget heterogeneously across layers. Its name draws a metaphor between separating a dense FFN into experts and separating a light beam into its spectrum. Spectral-MoE consists of two distinct approaches: a static approach that uses a single per-layer scalar which is shared across tokens, and a dynamic approach which emits a per-token schedule. We demonstrate that both allocators improve performance over uniform on both the analytic-based and learning-based SOTA dense-to-MoE methods. At 25% FFN utilization, dynamic allocation reduces WikiText-2 perplexity relative to uniform by 1.04, 2.96, and 2.71 points on the learned base for Llama-2-7B, Llama-3-8B, and Qwen2.5-7B, respectively. The corresponding reductions on the analytic base are 2.45, 6.26, and 2.17 points. On the learned base, dynamic allocation improves six-task mean accuracy over uniform by an average of 1.5, 4.2, and 3.6 percentage points across the three models at FFN utilizations of 12.5%, 25%, and 50%, respectively. These results establish per-layer expert utilization as a novel optimization axis in dense-to-MoE conversion.

Title: LLMs program using Reusable and Composable Abstract Representations

  • Authors: Casper L. Christensen, Kyle J. Ray, Dmitry Manning-Coe, Adam Shai, Paul Riechers
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.PL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19238
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19238
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19238]] LLMs program using Reusable and Composable Abstract Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19238)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: When writing code, the same computation can be expressed in different programming languages and through different implementation strategies. We show that the activations of LLMs capture this shared structure in reusable and composable abstract representations when they generate code. These representations emerge in the middle layers, are reusable across programming languages, and afford the model an impressive amount of generalization ability when generating code under various interventions. Using activation steering, we show that function, programming language, and implementation strategy can be independently manipulated. The independent interventions on language and function can be flexibly composed to control code generation, even in cases when the prompt contains no information about language and function. We additionally find that finetuning on examples of arbitrarily defined functions in one programming language transfers to other languages, consistent with the models reusing abstract features of code generation that are shared across different examples. Finally, to study how such abstract representations come to be, we implement a minimal model of shared, reusable, and composable structure in a synthetic setting, and show that transformers trained on such data learn representations that reuse these parts flexibly across contexts.

Title: Long-horizon Video Generation with Slot Memory Addressed By Region Tokens

  • Authors: Eren Cetin, Julius Erbach, Vukasin Bozic, Hyojun Go, Zixiang Zhao, Nikolai Kalischek, Federico Tombari, Konrad Schindler, Dominik Narnhofer
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19257
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19257
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19257]] Long-horizon Video Generation with Slot Memory Addressed By Region Tokens(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19257)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Memory for long-horizon video generation must satisfy two competing requirements: it must remain bounded so generation can continue indefinitely, while still recovering what was observed at a place when the camera returns, regardless of how long ago it was seen. Reconstruction-based memories provide spatial recall by caching and reprojecting explicit geometry, but require growing storage and depend on estimated 3D structure. Recurrent memories keep a constant-size state, but retention is governed by temporal recurrence, causing old observations to fade with time. We introduce SMART, a slot memory addressed by region tokens, which combines bounded storage with spatially addressed recall. SMART stores content in a constant-size slot recurrence whose writes are addressed by the observing camera and whose reads are queried by the rendering camera, without constructing an explicit 3D representation or maintaining a growing cache. Consequently, recall is conditioned on inter-view geometry rather than on recency. Each read additionally provides a confidence estimate that conditions a chunk-causal diffusion transformer, allowing it to selectively trust retrieved content while relying on its generative prior elsewhere. On long trajectories in Memory Maze and Minecraft, SMART maintains high-fidelity recall far beyond its training horizon while using constant memory and per-step cost.

  • Abstract: Agent skills are Markdown documents that a tool-using agent reads as instructions and then acts on with the privileges of the session that loaded them. Title: Outcome-Routed Distillation and Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Wentao Lu, Tianyu Zhu, Jesse Clark
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19271

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: On-policy distillation provides dense supervision on the student's own samples, but its targets still come from the teacher. Combining the two is difficult for diffusion language models (dLLMs), because bidirectional denoising makes the policy likelihoods required by ratio-based RL unreliable. We develop a likelihood-free pipeline for a mixture-of-experts dLLM. Each rollout group is routed by verifier outcome. Mixed groups use ratio-free, group-centred REINFORCE. Its tool-calling score differs by -0.16 points, with a confidence interval that includes zero, although this comparison also removes a small fallback distillation route. A domain-conditional recipe retains a 16.37-point tool-calling gain; a second seed gives 18.23 points and reproduces both preservation results, but the math result is seed-dependent. We pre-registered every arm and evaluated each comparison on paired items in a single sweep on one machine.

Title: Data-free Multistep Diffusion Distillation

  • Authors: Ngoc Duy Tran, Kien Do, Duc Thanh Nguyen, Truyen Tran
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19305

  • Abstract: One-step diffusion distillation achieves strong performance on small datasets but remains insufficient for high-resolution or complex text-to-image settings. Extending to multistep distillation often requires training data, suffers from early-step degradation, inconsistent outputs across sampling budgets, and incurs high computational cost. We identify that these limitations stem from simultaneous training, where a single adapted network approximates multiple score functions of different student distributions at once, causing biased distribution matching and unstable optimization. Therefore, we propose Progressive Multistep Data-free Distillation (PMDD), which assigns a dedicated adapted network per student distribution while avoiding the memory overhead of maintaining multiple adapted networks. Extensive experiments show that PMDD matches or outperforms data-free few-step baselines at every multistep budget, reaches FID 1.99 on ImageNet 64x64 in two steps, below its 512-NFE teacher (2.32), and achieves FID 7.76 on zero-shot COCO 512x512, while requiring 4-5x less training compute than data-free DMDv2 to support three inference budgets.

Title: Why don't Graph Transformers attend to the same token?

  • Authors: Alexandre Bismuth
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19324
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19324
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19324]] Why don't Graph Transformers attend to the same token?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19324)
  • Keywords: protect, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: In large language models (LLMs), attention concentrates heavily on the first token. This 'attention sink' is believed to protect deep models against over-mixing. Even though graph transformers face a related pressure, we find that a SoTA graph transformer, GRIT, rarely forms sinks on molecules, and that it sinks less when trained on larger graphs, the opposite of the trend in LLMs. In principle, a graph can prevent sinks: we prove and show empirically that its symmetry and locality limit the attention any node can receive. On molecules, however, with distinguishable atoms and global attention, neither limit prevents sinks; what stops them lies in the model. We find that the attention scoring function is the main cause. Replacing GRIT's gated logit with a dot product raises its sink rate from 0.09 to 0.68 on identical graphs. In Graph Transformers using dot-product attention, removing the sink gives about the same error as removing a random node, while GRIT's most-attended nodes matter to its predictions. Attention sinks are therefore a property of dot-product attention rather than of Transformers. In our experiments, they form when dot-product scoring has a distinguishable node to concentrate on.

  • Abstract: Privacy-preserving machine learning has become critical for deploying artificial intelligence in sensitive domains such as healthcare, finance, and infrastructure monitoring. Title: Asymptotic Behavior of Transformers

  • Authors: Katy Blumer, Da-Cheng Juan, Jon Kleinberg, Ravi Kumar, Cyrus Rashtchian, Andrew Tomkins
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.ST
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19358

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19358]] Asymptotic Behavior of Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19358)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: In this paper we study the behavior of transformer networks generating text over long periods of time. We show that even a three-token, single-layer attention-only transformer can fail to converge. We then study a rich class of transformers with arbitrary finite depth, layer normalization, MLPs, RoPE encodings, and an arbitrary mix of local and global layers. Every transformer in this class has a softmax temperature threshold above which its empirical cache distribution converges to a unique limit, independent of initial conditions. Title: Tail Competition Explains Scaling in Best-of-N Sampling for Verifiable Problems

  • Authors: Taro Yano, Yoichi Ishibashi, Masafumi Oyamada
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19362
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19362
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19362]] Tail Competition Explains Scaling in Best-of-N Sampling for Verifiable Problems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19362)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Best-of-N sampling is a simple inference-time scaling strategy for large language models, yet its behavior under incomplete reward models remains poorly understood. We study Best-of-N as a competition between the extreme reward tails of correct and incorrect responses. This view shows that success depends not only on the probability of sampling a correct response, but also on whether correct responses dominate incorrect ones in the upper tail. Using extreme value theory, we characterize three asymptotic regimes: correct-tail-dominant, incorrect-tail-dominant, and critical explaining both monotonic gains and reward-hacking-induced degradation. We further derive finite-sample scaling laws for representative reward distributions, showing that, under Gaussian rewards, incorrect responses with sufficiently large variance can dominate selection even when their mean reward is lower. Unlike oracle selection, convergence with an incomplete selector can be polynomial rather than exponential. Finally, we propose a lightweight tail-tension criterion to estimate when additional sampling may become harmful. Experiments on synthetic data and real reward model scores are consistent with the theoretically predicted regimes, scaling laws, and diagnostic behavior, suggesting that tail competition provides a unified lens on Best-of-N sampling for verifiable problems.

Title: Policy-Verified Self-Training: Learning Agent Compliance from Verifiable Policy Rewards

  • Authors: Min-Young Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19372
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19372

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Runtime policy verifiers can prevent agents from executing disallowed actions, but their decisions are typically used only for enforcement and discarded afterward. We ask whether these decisions can instead become verifiable policy rewards for training the agents they guard. We introduce Policy-Verified Self-Training (PVST), a post-training paradigm in which an agent generates its own interaction trajectories and an executable policy verifier, here a deterministic gate compiled from a written policy, evaluates whether each action complies. Compliant trajectories are reinforced, and violations become negatives for preference optimization; beyond the gate and the benchmark's task-success signal, PVST needs no stronger teacher model, no human preference labels and no attack-specific supervision, although rollouts are collected in the benchmark's attacked environment. The learned behavior holds under an attack template never used in training (37.7% to 11.0%) and on a suite excluded from training (34.8% to 16.7%), and the retained verifier's intervention rate falls from 39.2% to 8.8% (seed 0). Our analysis identifies two conditions under which the verdict is most informative: success must not already imply compliance, and guarded actions must appear in both permitted and forbidden contexts.

  • Abstract: We seek a hierarchical resolution of residual error under a composite learning objective as a principled mechanism for identifying and exploiting additional information, particularly under distribution shift and perturbation.   • Abstract: We study continuous global optimization problems in which the objective function cannot be directly evaluated; rather, we observe only which of two selected design points appears to perform better, subject to noise.   • Authors: Zhaoyan Jia, Chengwei Zhou, Gourav Datta, Massoud Pedram

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19408

  • Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have advanced rapidly, yet strengthening their reasoning through reinforcement learning (RL) remains an open challenge. Any-order generation can suffer from entropy degradation, which narrows the reasoning paths available for exploration. Recent work mitigates this issue by applying standard GRPO with left-to-right generation. However, independently generating every rollout from the input prompt can leave alternative continuations from intermediate states underexplored, while trajectory-level advantages assign uniform credit to all tokens without distinguishing the contributions of individual reasoning segments. We propose ForkGRPO, an RL framework that couples efficient exploration with fine-grained segment-level credit assignment. It samples multiple continuations from high-entropy intermediate states along a reference trajectory, prioritizing textual boundaries and reusing shared prefixes. The continuations provide complementary learning signals: within-state group-relative advantages guide which continuations to reinforce, while Monte Carlo state-value differences indicate which reasoning segments improve expected outcomes. Among the evaluated architecture-preserving RL methods, ForkGRPO achieves the best performance on three of four benchmarks, while improving over the directly comparable JustGRPO by up to 1.2 percentage points and using up to 27.8% fewer generated tokens. ForkGRPO-Fast further achieves a $2.49\times$ training wall-clock speedup with negligible accuracy loss.

  • Authors: Yifei Sun, Fenglei Fan, Junhao Jia, Wenming Deng, Hongxia Xu, Changmiao Wang, Ruiquan Ge

  • Abstract: Chest X-Ray (CXR) is a primary modality for diagnosing pulmonary diseases, yet the overlap of bony structures often obscures critical radiographic findings, increasing the risk of diagnostic uncertainty. While Dual-Energy Subtraction (DES) is the clinical benchmark for bone suppression, its widespread adoption is primarily hindered by specialized hardware requirements and increased radiation exposure. Recently, diffusion-based generative models have shown promise for this task, yet they are still constrained by reconstruction-biased latent compression, prior-induced domain gap, and a scarcity of high-quality paired datasets. By incorporating a multi-level depth feature loss derived from the pretrained Depth Anything V2 encoder, DAE preserves both visual fidelity and structural consistency. Second, we formulate bone suppression as a source-conditioned Brownian bridge diffusion process, leveraging CXRs as both the diffusion prior and structural guidance to narrow the source-to-target domain gap and maintain anatomical plausibility. Furthermore, we have constructed and released SZCH-X-Rays, the largest publicly available high-quality paired dataset for bone suppression to date, comprising 741 pairs of CXR and DES soft tissue images. Extensive experiments and downstream evaluation demonstrate that DeBoneDiT achieves superior bone suppression performance with reduced computational overhead.

Title: ActICL: Post-Training for Agentic Multimodal In-Context Learning

  • Authors: Yanshu Li, Ruixiang Tang, Junyi Jessy Li, Elias Stengel-Eskin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19475
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19475
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19475]] ActICL: Post-Training for Agentic Multimodal In-Context Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19475)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal in-context learning (ICL) is an important capability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Its performance is closely tied to the demonstrations it uses, making demonstration retrieval a core problem for ICL. Common methods use fixed scoring methods to retrieve all the demonstrations at once, which may introduce redundancy and noise. Context-aware retrieval methods have emerged to address this problem by allowing incremental retrieval from a growing context. However, none of them lets the MLLM learn how to judge what new demonstrations it needs and then carry out progressive retrieval on its own. To this end, we introduce ActICL, which trains an MLLM agent to acquire and use an ICL context via tool-augmented reasoning. An ActICL agent analyzes its own information gaps during iterative reasoning and tool-use actions. Based on these gaps, it performs targeted retrieval to acquire a set of initial candidates. It can then use the existing context to further filter the candidates and refine the context, or it can iteratively perform a new retrieval. When the agent judges that the context is sufficient for the task, or when the resource limit is reached with the user request met, it sends the acquired context to an answering model, either the agent itself or another MLLM. We use a two-stage post-training procedure that jointly optimizes the agent's acquisition and answering. With an adaptive number of demonstrations, ActICL improves average performance by 11.6 percentage points over standard similarity-based retrieval across seven benchmarks and three MLLMs, and by 4.4 points compared to the strongest prior baseline. The contexts acquired by ActICL agents can also be used effectively by frontier models. When GPT-5.6-Sol performs 4-shot ICL, ActICL's contexts improve its performance by 3.5 points over standard retrieval, averaged over three benchmarks.

Title: What You Sign Is Not What You Run: The Verifiability Gap in Quantized LLM Supply Chains

  • Authors: Wei-Liang Liu, Cheng-Yen Tsai
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19498
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19498
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19498]] What You Sign Is Not What You Run: The Verifiability Gap in Quantized LLM Supply Chains(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19498)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Model signing authenticates the full-precision weights W an LLM is released with, yet users run a quantized artifact Q whose producing transformation is not uniquely determined under current disclosure. Across the 200 most-downloaded Hugging Face GGUF repositories (198 in scope), no artifact or model card records the build environment, so under a recorded-inputs protocol the IVR is 0/198. Yet the artifacts are not irreproducible. An older release additionally pins the instruction set and did not re-derive. Reproducing the divergence reported with this http URL PR #25353, we show that E is a necessary coordinate and that one compiler setting yields a unique output across the tested platforms at negligible quality cost (+0.06% perplexity). As a security characterization, benign floating-point non-determinism separates from uniformly placed nibble-flip tampering above a detection floor that grows with the environments a verifier must allow, but a targeted embedding edit evades the statistic, so the proposed protocol rests on exact re-derivation; adversarial-fp16 attacks lie outside it. We release the audit data and a re-runnable harness.

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Block-diffusion language models denoise a whole block of token positions in parallel so each forward pass can emit many tokens. However, blocks are still denoised in strict sequence, a cost that within-block acceleration leaves untouched. Here, we investigate decoding blocks concurrently and propose One Block Ahead (OBA), a training-free decoding algorithm that denoises the next block alongside the current one. We demonstrate that for uniform-state diffusion LMs, where a block's denoising progress is not visible in its intermediate tokens, concurrent decoding is advantageous if the next block's tokens are promoted together with the corresponding sampler state, including the self-conditioning signal and the denoising time. We also derive a break-even criterion that relates the benefit of OBA to the relative cost of a paired-block forward pass. On DiffusionGemma, a state-of-the-art diffusion LM, OBA increases the tokens per forward pass by 42.3% (from 15.64 to 22.25) and the single-request denoising throughput by 16.3%, averaged over ten benchmarks, with a small accuracy regression.

  • Abstract: Typed decision models are language models that answer a question with a fixed set of possible answers by returning a probability for each answer instead of generating text. Every request is sent in its original form and under 21 types of rewrite that keep its content, such as renaming the options or writing the table in another format. Decision models are a practical choice for tabular decisions, but their robustness remains an open problem.

  • Abstract: Lipreading remains challenging due to the inherent ambiguity of visual speech cues and their sensitivity to head pose and video resolution. During pretraining, auxiliary audio input is incorporated to facilitate cross-modal alignment, and modality dropout is applied to encourage robust visual-only representations. Evaluated on LRS2, LRS3, and the out-of-domain WildVSR benchmark, FLARE achieves strong lipreading performance, with word error rates of 11.7%, 13.2%, and 34.3%, respectively. A progressive design study confirms the contribution of each major component. Further analyses demonstrate that joint face-lip modeling improves robustness under degraded video resolution and large head pose variation, while yielding more phoneme-discriminative visual representations. Code and pretrained models are available at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Safety alignment of text-to-image diffusion models aims to suppress harmful content without compromising useful generation capabilities. Title: Moral Combat: Exploiting Moral Foundations Theory to Jailbreak LLMs

  • Authors: Adel Khorramrouz, Yi-Chia Wang, Sharon Levy
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19587
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19587
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19587]] Moral Combat: Exploiting Moral Foundations Theory to Jailbreak LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19587)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Social psychology has long described how people can be persuaded to interpret a harmful act as moral. Recent research suggests that Large Language Models (LLMs) echo aspects of human psychology, from cognitive biases to social conformity. This raises a question with direct safety implications: can LLMs be moralized to bypass safety guardrails? We answer this question with a multi-agent framework grounded in Moral Foundations Theory (MFT). Given a harmful request that is initially refused, our agents diagnose which moral foundation (MF) the prompt is violating, identify a justifiable foundation under which the same request appears virtuous, rewrite the prompt accordingly, and reinforce it with generated precedents of comparable moral reframing. We evaluate our framework on harmful prompts from three well-known jailbreak datasets across five open-source and proprietary models. Our analysis reveals that models are highly susceptible to moralization, exhibiting high single-trial jailbreak rates with success increasing further under repeated attempts. We also find that safety benchmarks are imbalanced in the MFs they violate, concentrating on foundations toward which models already exhibit biases.

Title: Beyond Static Positional Encoding: Bayesian Time-aware Attention for Non-Stationary Long-term Time Series Forecasting

  • Authors: Xinjie Lan, Yunbo Mei, Leheng Zhou, Hao Qi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19603
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19603
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19603]] Beyond Static Positional Encoding: Bayesian Time-aware Attention for Non-Stationary Long-term Time Series Forecasting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19603)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers have demonstrated strong performance in long-term time series forecasting (LTSF), yet modeling non-stationary temporal dependencies remains challenging. In this paper, we interpret self-attention as a Bayesian posterior over attended positions, combining a content-based prior induced by scaled dot-product similarity with a temporal-evidence likelihood induced by positional encoding. This formulation provides a probabilistic interpretation of how positional encoding shapes the temporal dependencies captured by self-attention. Building on this perspective, we derive a population-level evidence lower bound (ELBO) that characterizes the role of positional encoding in forecasting weakly stationary time series. Our analysis further highlights a limitation of static positional encoding: when temporal dependence structures vary across input windows, a fixed temporal bias must accommodate heterogeneous dynamics and can therefore be suboptimal for non-stationary forecasting. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Bayesian Time-aware Attention (BTA), which employs a mixture-of-experts (MoE) router to adaptively combine temporal kernels and generate input-conditioned, head-specific positional biases. Extensive experiments on standard LTSF benchmarks demonstrate that BTA consistently improves forecasting performance across multiple Transformer backbones and achieves competitive overall performance with modest computational overhead and stable training.

Title: PN-Mix: Pair-Norm Guided Mixed-Precision Key Cache Quantization for Efficient Long-Output LLM Inference

  • Authors: Gil Jae Song, Sungjoo Yoo
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19605
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19605
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19605]] PN-Mix: Pair-Norm Guided Mixed-Precision Key Cache Quantization for Efficient Long-Output LLM Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19605)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Long-output inference in large language models suffers from severe KV cache memory and bandwidth bottlenecks. While KV cache quantization alleviates this issue, low-bit quantization (e.g., 2-bit) severely degrades model accuracy. Recent mixed-precision key-cache quantization methods mitigate this degradation by allocating higher precision to important key channels, but estimate channel saliency using only key statistics. Through attention logit perturbation analysis at the RoPE channel-pair level, we show that quantization errors in channel pairs with large query--key pair-norm products contribute significantly to logit variation. Based on this insight, we propose PN-Mix, a mixed-precision key-cache quantization method for RoPE-based decoder-only LLMs, which allocates higher precision to salient channel pairs identified once during the prefill stage and reused throughout decoding. Across standard reasoning, code-generation, and AIME benchmarks, PN-Mix with 2.26-bit average precision achieves comparable or better average accuracy than prior mixed-precision methods using 2.32--2.39 bits. Furthermore, in our Pareto sweep, PN-Mix degrades gracefully down to 1.5 bits, whereas the compared baselines exhibit sharper performance drops below 2.25 bits.

Title: A Graph-Based Analysis of Comparability, Scale, and Attribution in Private Transformer Inference

  • Authors: Souvik Das, Ariful Azad, Nitesh Saxena
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19607
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19607
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19607]] A Graph-Based Analysis of Comparability, Scale, and Attribution in Private Transformer Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19607)
  • Keywords: privacy, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Rising privacy concerns around large language models have accelerated research in private Transformer inference (PTI). Synthesizing the empirical claims in this line of research, however, is hindered by multi-dimensional cryptographic progress and inconsistent evaluation contracts. In this work, we systematize the field by constructing an empirical graph of 62 PTI systems, mapping system attributes to node features and evaluation compatibility to edges. A three-tiered analysis of this graph illuminates the state of private inference. Node-level attribution shows that cryptographic progress drives performance gains across most systems, yet always co-occurs with other factors (model fidelity, hardware, cost accounting). Finally, graph-level analysis shows that the scaling frontier of private inference has plateaued at 13B parameters and projects the cost of deploying current PTI techniques on production-scale Transformers. Overall, this graph-based framework equips the community to evaluate PTI claims transparently and channel technical speedups into deployment-scale private inference.

Title: Are We Really Measuring Forgetting? Pitfalls in Evaluating Machine Unlearning for Multimodal LLMs

  • Abstract: As machine unlearning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) rapidly advances, rigorous evaluation of its effectiveness becomes increasingly important. In this work, we identify three major pitfalls that may lead to misleading conclusions: the lack of heldout evaluation to distinguish genuine concept-wise forgetting from instance-wise overfitting, the reliance on synthetic, fine-tuned knowledge rather than real-world knowledge acquired during pre-training, and unfair method comparisons caused by applying the same training settings to different unlearning methods.
