Title: Self-Conditioning Enables Deep Sequential Reasoning in Flow Matching

  • Authors: Zeyang Zhang, Linxiang Gao, Meiqi Gu, Xingyan Chen, Minrui Luo, Kaifeng Lyu, Tianxing He
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19628
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19628
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19628]] Self-Conditioning Enables Deep Sequential Reasoning in Flow Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19628)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Recent work has explored visual reasoning through image generation, where models solve visual-reasoning problems by directly generating solution images. Intuitively, iterative samplers such as flow matching are well suited to this task, since each sampling step may resemble one reasoning step. However, in this work, from a circuit complexity perspective, we show that this intuition is misleading. We prove that as long as the model outputs satisfy certain mild regularity conditions, the trajectory of the vanilla flow-matching sampler can be simulated by a constant number of sequential rounds of parallel model evaluations. Consequently, additional sampling steps cannot increase the expressive power of a constant-depth backbone model under the standard complexity-theoretic model of transformers. We then reveal that self-conditioning, which conditions the model on the previous step's estimate of the final image, plays a previously unrecognized role in removing this bottleneck. Controlled experiments on diverse visual-reasoning tasks show that flow matching with self-conditioning achieves higher accuracy and greater reasoning depth than vanilla flow matching, and its performance improves with more sampling steps. Theoretically, we prove that with self-conditioning, a constant-depth backbone can achieve expressive power equivalent to that of space-bounded Turing machines. Our study suggests a design principle for future visual reasoning research: carry more information between sampling steps, so that additional steps translate into deeper reasoning.

Combined with a tailored multi-stage optimization scheme, the proposed MicroSDF achieves robust and high-fidelity reconstruction across reflective and non-reflective surfaces. Title: Beyond State Capacity: Why Retrieval Breaks in Mamba

  • Authors: Nir Yarden, Amit Ben-Artzy, Roy Schwartz
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19709
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19709
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19709]] Beyond State Capacity: Why Retrieval Breaks in Mamba(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19709)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Mamba models underperform Transformers on in-context retrieval. This gap is usually attributed to Mamba's fixed-size recurrent state, which loses information as the context grows. We challenge this assumption by focusing on query-first retrieval, a setup in which the query precedes the context and state capacity should not be the binding constraint. We show that even in this setting Mamba lags substantially behind Transformers, despite its selection mechanism, which was designed to distinguish relevant content from distractors. We then demonstrate that the Mamba selection gates have sufficient control over the state updates to influence the model output: intervening on specific gates alone can swing accuracy between extremes. However, as gates are input-dependent, they require the incoming token's representation to encode its relevance to the query. We therefore probe token representations across layers, and find that the relevance signal emerges only gradually with model depth, and that the gates begin to reflect it only several layers deeper. Crucially, we find that gates in retrieval-critical layers are not yet aligned with relevance. The selection mechanism is thus expressive enough to solve the task, but it commonly operates on a relevance signal that has not yet arrived. Mamba's retrieval bottleneck therefore extends beyond state capacity: even when capacity is sufficient, the delayed emergence of relevance information across depth often prevents successful retrieval.

Title: Mitigating Video Hallucinations via Attention-Guided Representation Perturbation

  • Authors: Zhixin Cai, Ruoqi Yuan, Xinchen Yang, Zhuofan Chen, Jun Bai, Chuantao Yin, Wenge Rong
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19728
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19728
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19728]] Mitigating Video Hallucinations via Attention-Guided Representation Perturbation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19728)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Video large language models (Video LLMs) often generate responses unsupported by the input video. Contrastive decoding (CD) can mitigate these hallucinations, but its effectiveness depends on how the negative branch weakens visual evidence. Existing methods rely on predefined perturbations or masking guided by question reconstruction, offering limited control over the model's visual reliance during answer generation. We introduce Attention-guided Representation Perturbation for Contrastive Decoding (ARP-CD), a training-free framework for constructing contrastive negatives directly in representation space. ARP-CD uses the attention mass assigned to visual tokens at the answer-prediction position to guide bounded gradient updates of the language model's input visual hidden states. These updates reduce visual attention and produce a negative input, whose predictions are contrasted with those of the original branch throughout generation. Experiments across four Video LLMs and four benchmarks show that ARP-CD outperforms representative training-free baselines in most model--benchmark settings, reducing hallucinations while improving general video understanding. Further analyses show that attention-guided perturbation directions improve negative quality and that the resulting negatives provide effective contrastive references for correcting the original predictions.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Autonomous navigation of planetary rovers is challenged by visually repetitive, featureless terrain. Title: Learning to Compute Is Not Learning to Retain or Reuse in Looped Transformers

  • Authors: Peilong Zhou, Hongtao Zhang, Yulong Huang, Cangyuan Li, Mingyu Chen, Ying Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19768
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19768
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19768]] Learning to Compute Is Not Learning to Retain or Reuse in Looped Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19768)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped transformers compute by applying one block repeatedly, so they can run for more loops than they were trained with. Recurrent reasoners are known to overthink: iterating past the trained depth can turn a correct answer into a wrong one. Such a late error can reflect failed computation, loss of retention, or a state that later computation cannot reuse. We separate these cases in in-context Mealy-machine execution, where a simulator supplies intermediate targets and exact counterfactuals for hidden-state interventions. Models trained to answer at loop 32 are fully correct there but keep only 22% of these answers at loop 64. Retention depends on what training asks for: supervising one randomly chosen position per example, instead of always the last, keeps 99.8%, and randomizing the number of training loops also helps; truncating gradients, as in progressive training against overthinking, does not. The computation itself is exact: position $k$ is computed at loop $k$, and swapping its loop-$k$ hidden vector for the one from another input redirects every later position to that input's exact continuation (237/237 pairs in each of ten models). Retention, however, is not reuse. Even in models that keep nearly all answers, holding each position $k$ fixed from loop $k$ drops accuracy to 4--6%, whereas holding it from loop $k+4$ is harmless. A released recurrent maze network trained to avoid overthinking behaves the same way. Learning to compute is thus not learning to retain, and avoiding overthinking does not make a state reusable when its answer first appears.

Title: From Global to Tail Convergence: Dynamics of $α$-Stable Score-Based Generative Models

  • Authors: Ianis Hammani (ENS Paris Saclay, LMO), Claire Boyer (LMO, IUF)
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.PR, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19773
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19773
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19773]] From Global to Tail Convergence: Dynamics of $α$-Stable Score-Based Generative Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19773)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Score-based generative models driven by $\alpha$-stable L{é}vy processes have been proposed as an alternative to Brownian diffusion models, with empirical evidence suggesting improved sampling and better modeling of heavy-tailed data. Yet theoretical guarantees for these models remain limited. We develop a non-asymptotic convergence analysis for scale-preserving $\alpha$-stable generative dynamics, covering both compactly supported and power-law targets. In p-Wasserstein distance, we quantify the effects of early stopping, initialization, score approximation, and time discretization, and show exponential decay of the initialization error. A comparison with Brownian dynamics reveals that global Wasserstein convergence alone does not fundamentally distinguish the two models: both initialization errors decay exponentially with the forward horizon on the common class of heavy-tailed targets. A sharp separation emerges instead in the tails. At any positive generation time, stable dynamics produces power-law tails with index $\alpha$, whereas Brownian dynamics remains light-tailed. For a target with tail index $\nu$, asymptotically correct extreme-quantile scaling is possible precisely when $\alpha$=$\nu$. These results identify tail-index matching as a principled mechanism behind the ability of stable generative models to capture extreme events.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19802
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19802]] Asymptotically Exact Negative Guidance of Diffusion Models via Positive-Unlabeled Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19802)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Negative guidance of diffusion models generates samples that avoid an undesired condition, specified either by reference samples or by a conditional score alongside an unconditional model. Existing methods inject repulsive terms derived from local information at each sample, so the resulting sampler does not target an explicit density, and its bias cannot be systematically reduced by increasing computation. We instead present a negative-guidance method that samples from an explicit target density, which uniformly handles both reference-sample and score-based specifications and suppresses probability mass near the undesired condition with an avoidance strength that parametrizes the target density itself. Realizing this target requires estimating, at each diffusion timestep, how likely each sample is to belong to the undesired condition. We cast this estimation as a Positive-Unlabeled learning problem that admits lightweight online training along the diffusion trajectory, using condition samples as positives and diffusion particles as unlabeled data. Because the guided particles drift from the unconditional marginal, we further correct the residual mismatch via Sequential Monte Carlo, yielding a sampler that is asymptotically exact in the particle limit and that turns any existing negative-guidance method into its proposal kernel. Empirically, our method approaches the ground-truth target on Gaussian mixtures and Pareto-dominates existing methods on trade-offs between avoidance and generation quality, distributional bias, and diversity, including in an image-generation setting with a pretrained score network.

Title: Adaptive and Proximal Federated Optimization for Transferable Network Intrusion Detection

  • Authors: Rezwan Islam Salvi, MD Rafin Jawad Hafiz, Abeer Mahmood Kalam, Md Moniruzzaman
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19808
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19808
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19808]] Adaptive and Proximal Federated Optimization for Transferable Network Intrusion Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19808)
  • Keywords: attack, robust, federate
  • Abstract: Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS) based on deep learning often fail to detect attack classes that are absent from training data, limiting their robustness against zero-day threats. This property called transferability across attack classes, remains underexplored especially in federated learning (FL) settings where data heterogeneity and client drift pose additional challenges. Prior work (TabFIDS) showed that FedAvg combined with bootstrapping and temporal averaging improves transferability for cross-device NIDS. In this paper, we explore whether other federated optimizers can do better. We compare two optimizers inspired by FedAdam and FedProx against the FedAvg baseline on the CIC-IDS 2017 dataset using an 11-client federated setup, sweep 10 hyperparameter configurations, and contrast two data-balancing strategies: bootstrapping and SMOTE. Using the count of client pairs whose transferred-model accuracy exceeds 70% as our primary metric (following TabFIDS), the proximal optimizer with a strong regularization term yields 48 transferable pairs versus 42 for FedAvg, while the adaptive optimizer with an aggressive server learning rate yields 46 pairs. SMOTE improves per-pair accuracy over bootstrapping but reduces breadth, revealing a depth-vs-breadth trade-off: high accuracy on a few client pairs versus moderate accuracy across most. These results indicate that the choice of federated optimizer, the rule by which client updates are regularized and aggregated into the global model, is very important for federated NIDS.

Title: VaryST: Generalizable Spatio-Temporal Forecasting Beyond the Closed-World Setting

  • Authors: Shengnan Guo, Jinjun Cheng, Xiaowei Mao, Jianhao Zhang, Haochen Lv, Yang Jiang, YiHeng Huang, Yan Lin, Youfang Lin, Huaiyu Wan

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Spatio-temporal forecasting is fundamental to many real-world systems, yet deployment environments are inherently varying: temporal distributions may evolve, spatial domains may change, and observation availability may shift from training conditions. Existing models are often tightly coupled to their training environments through node-specific representations, predefined spatial support sets, and reliance on consistently available observations, limiting generalization to changing environments. To this end, we propose VaryST, a compact spatio-temporal forecasting model trained from scratch that achieves strong generalization by dynamically constructing predictive context from available long-term observations. To accommodate temporal changes, VaryST derives node representations from long-term histories, capturing current behavior in the context of longer-term patterns. To adapt to spatial changes, it dynamically selects functionally relevant support nodes beyond predefined neighborhoods and integrates their information with recent and long-term target states and global context. To handle varying observation availability, VaryST selectively masks historical patches on which the model relies most during training, encouraging it to exploit alternative temporal and spatial dependencies for accurate forecasting when key observations become unavailable. Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets against seventeen baselines show that VaryST achieves strong in-domain forecasting accuracy, substantially improves zero- and few-shot generalization under spatial and temporal changes, and remains robust to varying observation availability.

Title: When Does Depth Help? Understanding Layerwise Inference in Tabular Foundation Models

  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19814
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19814]] When Does Depth Help? Understanding Layerwise Inference in Tabular Foundation Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19814)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformer-based tabular foundation models (TFMs) achieve state-of-the-art performance on small to medium-sized tabular prediction tasks while processing diverse inputs through a shared stack of layers. Recent layerwise analyses identify strong predictive performance in early TFM representations and substantial overlap in computation across layers. However, early predictive accessibility alone does not establish whether later layers are dispensable for every input, and aggregate accuracy can obscure which inputs benefit from additional computation. We study all 24 in-context-learning layers of TabPFN-3 across 272 classification datasets, combining representation geometry, prediction trajectories, truncation, and structural interventions, while also examining related representation dynamics in four additional TFMs. We uncover a pronounced expansion-compression trajectory, with stronger late dimensional contraction associated with larger increases in class-prototype separation. A targeted analysis of early-stabilizing samples shows that substantial representational changes persist after predicted classes stabilize, while locally ambiguous queries obtain larger accuracy gains from shallow to late layers than well-separated queries. Repeating a single pretrained block or perturbing layer order reduces predictive performance on average, constraining simple post-hoc substitutions in the frozen model. These findings distinguish predictive accessibility, class stability, and the benefits of continuing computation, supporting an input-dependent assessment of depthwise redundancy in TFMs.

  • Abstract: LLMs absorb vast knowledge from training data, yet their inherently probabilistic nature offers no guarantee that this knowledge will surface in their outputs.   • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Continual learning aims to acquire new knowledge while retaining previously learned knowledge. However, updating shared parameters when learning new tasks often overwrites existing knowledge, causing catastrophic forgetting. Memory replay methods mitigate catastrophic forgetting but rely on previous samples, raising privacy, memory, and scalability concerns. On the other hand, parameter isolation-based methods preserve learned knowledge but may limit knowledge transfer, whereas regularization promotes adaptability but remains susceptible to interference between old and new tasks. To maintain the stability-plasticity trade-off, this paper proposes Stability-Plasticity-Aware Region Selection (SPARS), a replay-free hybrid framework combining global parameter isolation with localized parameter regularization. Based on parameter importance, SPARS partitions parameters into three importance-aware regions namely, winning, neighboring, and non-important parameter regions. Each region receives a different adaptation strategy: selective freezing, soft regularization, or unrestricted learning based on its role in the stability-plasticity trade-off. To mitigate classifier bias in class-incremental learning, we employ Quadratic Discriminant Analysis to improve performance on previously learned classes .Experiments on CIFAR-10 and TinyImageNet demonstrate consistent improvement in average incremental accuracy over existing isolation- and regularization-based methods across task- and class-incremental learning. On TinyImageNet, SPARS achieves the best performance with 6% improvement for class-incremental learning while retaining over 60% of parameters for future tasks.

Title: Bilinear Compressive Security with Finitely Many Samples

  • Authors: Axel Flinth, Semira Einsele, Matilda Kylberg, Hubert Orlicki, Gerhard Wunder
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.IT, eess.SP
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19898
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19898
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19898]] Bilinear Compressive Security with Finitely Many Samples(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19898)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Bilinear Compressive Security (BCS) is a lightweight encryption scheme that combines compressed sensing with a fresh random convolution filter to improve resistance against key-recovery attacks under key reuse. Previous work derived information-theoretic guarantees for BCS by relating key identifiability to phase retrieval under the assumption that an adversary has access to the full ciphertext distribution induced by the random filter. In practice, however, an adversary only observes finitely many encryptions, leaving the security of BCS in the finite-sample regime largely unexplored. In this paper, we study key recovery from only finitely many observations. We first develop a covariance-based attack that estimates the linear measurements from repeated encryptions of the same plaintext and subsequently recovers the secret measurement matrix. We analyze the estimation procedure and derive finite-sample guarantees together with corresponding sample-complexity bounds. We further investigate the complete attack experimentally and compare its observed recovery behavior with the previously established information-theoretic identifiability results. Our experiments show two qualitatively different recovery regimes on the structure of the measurement matrix. For dense matrices, including Gaussian matrices, the number of measurements required for successful recovery appears to grow linearly with the signal dimension. This differs from the worst-case behavior suggested by information theory. In contrast, for matrices with sparse rows, we observe a quadratic growth consistent with the theoretical bound. These results suggest that the sparsity structure of the measurement matrix plays an important role in the security of BCS and should be taken into account when selecting parameters and designing practical instances of the scheme.

Title: CARD: Closing the Teacher-Student Loop for Adversarial Robustness Distillation

  • Authors: Zhi Li, Haowei Liu, Hongchen Yang, Xiaoxuan Wang, Song Gao, Zhenli He, Wei Zhou, Shaowen Yao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19911
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19911
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19911]] CARD: Closing the Teacher-Student Loop for Adversarial Robustness Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19911)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Adversarial robustness distillation typically treats a robust teacher as a fixed knowledge source and transfers robustness to a compact student in a one-way manner. This paradigm terminates teacher-student interaction once the student is distilled, thereby discarding potentially useful feedback from the distilled student. However, exploiting such feedback is nontrivial because indiscriminate weak-to-strong transfer may propagate student errors to the teacher. To address this issue, we propose Cyclic Adversarial Robustness Distillation (CARD), a closed-loop framework that turns the distilled student into a feedback source for refining the robust teacher and subsequently transfers the teacher gains back to the same student. CARD first performs strong-to-weak adversarial distillation and then uses reliability-enhanced student feedback to refine the robust teacher. The refinement combines inverse adversarial student feedback with teacher-student discrepancy maximization, after which the refined teacher is used to continue training the same student under the unchanged base distillation objective. We further establish a sample-level sufficient condition for reliable weak-to-strong feedback transfer and derive a distribution-level upper bound on the refined teacher's risk. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet-10 across multiple teacher-student architectures and base distillation methods show that CARD improves adversarial robustness in most evaluated settings while generally preserving clean accuracy. These results show that the distilled student can provide useful feedback to the teacher, enabling robustness transfer to proceed beyond the conventional one-way teacher-to-student paradigm.

Such agreement estimates can support more reliable validation when labels are noisy or conflicting.

Title: Not All Criteria Matter: Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting for Rubric-Based LLM Evaluation

  • Authors: Haocheng Yang, Licheng Pan, Yiming Dong, Xiaoxi Li, Yuan Lu, Zhenhao Chen, Jiajun Fan, Shuai Shao, Chunyuan Zheng, Hao Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19915
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19915
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19915]] Not All Criteria Matter: Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting for Rubric-Based LLM Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19915)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Rubric-based evaluation assesses the responses of large language models (LLMs) by aggregating the verification results of explicit criteria, yet not all criteria matter equally. However, existing methods commonly aggregate the verification results with uniform weighting, which assigns minor and overlapping criteria the same weight as essential ones and thus produces misleading evaluation scores. Although some methods prompt an LLM to directly assign weights, direct weighting requires a holistic judgment over all criteria that LLMs struggle to make reliably. To address these limitations, we propose Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting (ICW), which derives the weights of criteria from pairwise importance comparisons made by an LLM. Specifically, ICW transforms the holistic judgment over all criteria into a series of pairwise importance comparisons, checks and revises inconsistent comparisons, and derives the weights from the principal eigenvector of the comparison matrix. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ICW consistently outperforms uniform weighting and competitive weighting methods, and translates into better policy models in downstream reinforcement learning.

Title: Quiet Triggers, Loud Footprints: A Tri-Space Measurement Study of Backdoor Stealthiness

  • Authors: Behrad Tajalli, Henk Berendsen, Xiaoyun Xu, Stjepan Picek
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19947
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19947
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.19947]] Quiet Triggers, Loud Footprints: A Tri-Space Measurement Study of Backdoor Stealthiness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19947)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, steal
  • Abstract: Backdoor attacks on deep neural networks are commonly evaluated by attack success rate and benign accuracy, yet their stealthiness remains inconsistently defined and measured. Existing work often studies only one observable aspect of stealthiness, such as trigger imperceptibility, feature-space separability, parameter-space concentration, or evasion of particular defenses. This creates a fragmented picture: an attack may appear stealthy under one metric while leaving conspicuous footprints elsewhere. This paper presents a tri-space measurement study of backdoor stealthiness in image classification. We distinguish latent stealthiness---the ideal but unobservable difficulty of distinguishing a backdoored system from a benign one---from measurable footprints in three spaces: input, feature, and parameter space. Under this framework, we evaluate representative backdoor attacks across four datasets and three model architectures using a broad suite of footprint metrics. Our results show that stealthiness is not monolithic: attacks that are quiet in one space are often loud in another, and existing attacks rarely achieve consistently small footprints across all three spaces. We further analyze agreement and disagreement among stealthiness metrics and study how footprint measurements interact with attack success rate. Based on observed blind spots in existing measurements, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: Class-Specific Davies--Bouldin Index (CDBI), which captures class-conditional feature overlap, and TAC--UCLC Product (TUP), which identifies channels that are both trigger-responsive and downstream influential. These metrics are intended to augment, not replace, the broader measurement suite. Finally, we provide practical guidance for future stealthiness evaluations.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: The number of attention heads in a transformer is usually set by convention or grid search. We estimate intrinsic dimension with a nested-dropout VAE trained on DistilBERT embeddings of WikiText-2, choosing the KL weight at the knee of the VAE's rate-distortion curve rather than by hand. We present the result as a practical heuristic worth testing at larger scale, not as a general principle.

From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths,evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades,logging, and an examiner who reads every script. Title: Gray-Box Hallucination Detection in LLMs with Token Identities

  • Authors: Shaked Goldshtein, Yitzchak Grunbaum, Fabrizio Frasca, Guy Bar-Shalom, Haggai Maron

They can therefore observe how uncertain the model is, but not what it is uncertain about. On the closed-source GPT-4.1 model, mean ROC-AUC increases from 67.39% to 71.84%. Among the three open-weight models, GURI outperforms transferred probability-only baseline in 17 of 18 directed cross-model transfers without fine-tuning. For the three open-weight models, cross-dataset transfer between HotpotQA and TriviaQA with the model held fixed improves mean ROC-AUC over transferred LOS-Net by 3.76 points.

Title: Provable Transformer Context Compression for In-Context Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Yajie Zhao, Yingjun Mo, Zhiyong Wang, Bo Tang, Chao Xu, Fang Kong
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20004

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers can perform in-context reinforcement learning without parameter updates. However, existing theoretical constructions often require context sizes that grow with the number of sampled transitions, leading to increasing memory costs in large-scale problems. This raises a fundamental scalability question: how much context is intrinsically necessary to carry out such learning? We answer this question for fixed-policy TD evaluation. We show that, with a shared linear decoder, the minimum context dimension required to exactly reproduce the first $L$ TD iterates is the Krylov dimension induced by the sampled transitions. When the available context dimension $m$ falls below this threshold, exact reproduction is no longer possible. Moving from representation complexity to actual memory cost, we characterize the optimal retained memory for exact continuous representations as $\Theta(nk_L)$ and show experimentally that on a sampled task whose exact rank is full but whose numerical rank is small, a context accurate to $10^{-2}$ is $88.7\%$ smaller than the sparse sufficient statistics of TD. Finally, we establish the learnability of the shared executor through pretraining by proving global convergence of an auxiliary objective over effective attention parameters under suitable task diversity. Experiments across diverse TD evaluation problems validate parameter recovery and the predicted context--accuracy tradeoff.

GBTSVR constructs nonparallel lower- and upper-bound regression functions over granular balls, significantly reducing sensitivity to sample-level perturbations. Building upon this framework, GB-TSVQR further incorporates quantile regression principles to simultaneously capture heterogeneous trends and asymmetric error distributions across different quantile levels. GDBA-Net adapts a frozen SAM2 encoder by integrating pretrained semantic representations with explicit frequency-derived structural priors. The complementary representations are independently transformed through Grid MLP branches and dynamically integrated using sample-adaptive channel-wise gating, selectively emphasizing structural cues relevant to nuclear delineation. Despite using only 4.3 million trainable parameters, GDBA-Net achieves state-of-the-art in-dataset performance and consistently superior cross-dataset generalization across six diverse nuclei segmentation benchmarks under fully supervised, cross-dataset, and five-shot adaptation protocols, outperforming competing CNN-, transformer-, and foundation model based methods. Comprehensive ablations confirm that these gains arise from the coordinated design of semantic-frequency complementarity and adaptive gating rather than increased model capacity. By uniting transferable semantic representations with explicit structural priors, GDBA-Net provides a robust, annotation-efficient solution to domain variability in nuclei segmentation. this https URL

  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning over decentralized networks inherently trades off the suppression of local finite-sample noise through collaboration against the bias that collaboration introduces between heterogeneous nodes.   • Abstract: Video scene graph generation (VidSGG) aims to capture objects and their evolving relationships in videos. As a result, a model may retain its previous prediction even after the underlying relation has changed. We refer to this failure mode as relational semantic inertia. Title: What Do Foundation Models for Market Microstructure Learn? A Controlled Study with Synthetic Limit Order Books

  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Autoregressive transformers trained on limit order book (LOB) message streams are increasingly proposed as foundation models for market microstructure. Because the generative process of real markets is unknown, these models can be judged only by the realism of their output, leaving open what they actually learn. We make the question answerable by inverting the setup: we train GPT-style models on order flow from simulators whose latent parameters are known, so that the Bayes-optimal predictor, the generating state, and the exact data entropy serve as references. The latent regime is linearly decodable from frozen activations, well above baselines, so a frozen-model read-out calibrates simulator parameters more accurately than windowed maximum likelihood at a fraction of the cost. In our experiments these capabilities are not produced by scale: capacity and token volume saturate early, and it is the diversity of training regimes, not the token count, that determines generalization to unseen markets. On real NASDAQ data, zero-shot transfer is set by how well the simulator prior covers real flow: an enriched mixture nearly closes the gap to an in-domain baseline, and pretraining beats from-scratch training at every budget across held-out tickers. Known ground truth turns average next-token loss, which is uninformative near the entropy floor, into an exact comparison against the Bayes-optimal and oracle predictors.

  • Abstract: Language models often produce semantically similar answers to open-ended questions, even when many plausible alternatives exist. Its objective, GapScore, combines query relevance (Demand) with dissimilarity to the nearest existing item (Defensibility). Component ablations expose the trade-off between semantic separation and relevance, while also showing that better direction scores do not uniformly yield better downstream metrics. These results support explicit gap search as a practical mechanism for diversifying content, with task validity checked separately from geometric novelty.

A lot of research is concerned with producing tools that either determine whether code is constant-time or that ensure it is compiled into a constant-time binary, with various degrees of accuracy. In this work, we present and evaluate a full workflow for hardening against timing side-channels during compilation. It starts with taint analysis to determine the code locations that depend on sensitive information. These are then the object of automated control-flow transformations inside the compiler to seal possible timing side-channels. We evaluate our workflow in detail on a realistic application running on a RISC-V core using both static timing analysis and statistical methods. Even though we do not reach cycle-accurate constant-time execution, our statistical results suggest that our hardened binary is safe against timing attacks.

Title: Secret Sharing from Non-Extractable Randomness

  • Authors: Divesh Aggarwal
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20173
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20173
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20173]] Secret Sharing from Non-Extractable Randomness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20173)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Bosley and Dodis (TCC 2007) proved that a class of imperfect randomness sources that suffices for information-theoretic encryption of messages longer than the logarithm of the key length must admit deterministic extraction of nearly uniform bits, and asked whether the same is true of two-out-of-two secret sharing. Aggarwal, Chung, Obremski, and Ribeiro (TCC 2022) gave a positive answer for schemes that are in addition leakage-resilient or non-malleable; the original question remained open. We answer it in the negative. For every $b\ge2$ we construct a class of flat sources on $\{0,1\}^{b+2}$, each of min-entropy at least $b$, together with a perfectly correct and perfectly private two-out-of-two scheme that shares a $b$-bit secret into two $(b+1)$-bit shares using any source in the class, and we show that every deterministic map from $\{0,1\}^{b+2}$ to $\{0,1\}$ is at statistical distance at least $1/6$ from uniform on some source in the class. This implies that sharing a long enough secret does not require extractable randomness. The proof is a short parity argument. The idea of the construction extends to other thresholds: for two-out-of-$n$ and $r$-out-of-$r$ sharing we give perfect schemes with $(b+1)$-bit shares, private for classes of flat sources from which no single bit can be extracted with error below $1/(4n-2)$ and $1/(2(2^r-1))$, respectively.

  • Abstract: Autoregressive generation underlies real-time, causal video synthesis and is the backbone of video world models. We show it can be made dramatically more efficient. We trace this to the teacher's trajectory geometry: the high-noise regime, governing semantic layout and motion, and the low-noise regime, governing fine texture exhibit fundamentally different curvature and optimization behavior, so a single monolithic student cannot jointly fit both, causing severe representation interference. This decoupling further reveals a general mechanism underlying few-step distillation: PF-ODE distillation mainly straightens the few-step trajectory geometry, while the subsequent distribution-matching stage independently governs distributional quality. Using only one PF-ODE trajectory, Glance Forcing already surpasses the fully-trained state-of-the-art baseline, raising the VBench Total score from 84.04 to 84.22 while cutting trajectory-collection cost from thousands of GPU-hours to about one A100-GPU-hour, with markedly larger gains in dynamic degree, VisionReward, and instruction-following, and consistent preference from human raters over strong baselines.

Title: MAEM: Mitigating Recency Bias via a Replay-Free, Capacity-Controlled Dynamic Mixture of Experts for Class-Incremental Graph Learning

  • Authors: Junlin Chen, Wenhao Wang, Junchao Xu, Qingsong Yao, Fei Xue, Li Sun, Xiao Wang, Ruijie Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20238
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20238
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20238]] MAEM: Mitigating Recency Bias via a Replay-Free, Capacity-Controlled Dynamic Mixture of Experts for Class-Incremental Graph Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20238)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) excel on graph-structured data but suffer catastrophic forgetting when classes arrive sequentially. In class-incremental graph learning, predictions are often dominated by recently learned classes, causing severe recency bias. Dynamic expansion can isolate knowledge across sessions, but many methods assume task-incremental settings and require task identities at test time to select branches or heads---information unavailable in class-incremental learning. Learnable routers offer an alternative, yet continual fine-tuning the router on new data can bias routing toward recent components. We propose MAEM, a replay-free, capacity-controlled dynamic mixture-of-experts framework for class-incremental graph learning. MAEM builds representations with Simplified Graph Convolution (SGC) and trains a dedicated expert for each new session, then freezes it to protect prior knowledge. For routing without task IDs or a shared trainable router, each expert uses a masked autoencoder (MAE); test nodes are assigned to the expert with the lowest reconstruction error, reducing routing-level recency bias. When the expert pool reaches a fixed capacity, MAEM merges the least-used experts via Git Re-Basin alignment and further distills them into a fused expert using topology-aware pseudo data synthesized from MAE distributions (DeepInversion-style), without storing or revisiting historical graph data. On four class-incremental graph benchmarks, MAEM mitigates recency bias, outperforms strong continual graph learning baselines, and keeps model capacity bounded under strict replay-free learning.

Nevertheless, the model selection approaches proposed in the literature suffer from a significant drop in accuracy when applied in Out-of-Distribution (OOD) settings. In this paper, we tackle the aforementioned limitation and propose RAMSAD, a retrieval-based framework for model selection in time-series anomaly detection. Given a new time series, our method queries a knowledge base of previously observed instances, retrieves the top-k most similar series, and transfers detector recommendations from their performance profiles. The framework can operate with standard similarity measures as well as embedding-based representations. Our results show that RAMSAD outperforms existing baselines in In-Distribution (ID) settings and is the most robust selector in OOD, where, when selecting a small subset of detectors, it matches or exceeds ensembles that run the entire detector pool, at a fraction of their cost. Overall, we demonstrate that similarity-based retrieval constitutes a strong and efficient foundation for model selection in time-series anomaly detection.

Title: P2Patch: Exploit-Guided Vulnerability Repair

  • Authors: Mohsen Iranmanesh, Sina Moradi Sabet, Sina Marefat, Ali Javidi Ghasr, Maxime Newman Nereyabagabo, Kousha Amouzesh, Jaivir Malhi, Vinci Velasco, Alex Chung, Mohammad A. Tayebi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20245
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20245
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20245]] P2Patch: Exploit-Guided Vulnerability Repair(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20245)
  • Keywords: security, attack, generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents for automated program repair, patching software vulnerabilities through multi-step reasoning and tool use. Yet these systems typically validate a patch against the same evidence used to construct it -- a static alert, sanitizer trace, published exploit, or test suite. A patch that blocks that one piece of evidence can therefore look successful while remaining vulnerable to the same vulnerability with some variation. We present P2Patch, a generative adversarial repair system that treats each patch as a new attack target. Starting from a normalized vulnerability finding, an exploitation agent constructs a reproducing proof of vulnerability (PoV), a patching agent repairs the program, and the exploitation agent then attacks the patched program for a distinct same-vulnerability exploit. Every successful exploit becomes a permanent repair requirement, and the final patch must block all PoVs accumulated during the patching process, with construction-time PoVs kept strictly separate from held-out evaluation oracles. Across 101 C/C++ and Java CVE subjects, this adversarial loop surfaces a distinct confirmed exploit in 41% of runs after the patch has already blocked its original construction-time PoV. Against single-pass repair from the same finding using Claude Code, P2Patch raises held-out fix-closed coverage from 78.1% to 84.7% and beyond-upstream coverage from 42.1% to 62.5%. Under the same held-out oracles, it also certifies more attack paths as blocked than recent state-of-the-art agentic repair systems on overlapping subjects. These results show that adversarial testing and independent security oracles expose repair gaps that one-shot validation misses.

Title: Contrastive uncertainty learning for robust multi-structure segmentation in lower-pelvic MR

  • Authors: Bilal Sidiqi, Yipei Wang, Wen Yan, Shaheer U. Saeed, Daniel C. Alexander, Yipeng Hu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20251

  • Abstract: Boundary delineation of anatomical structures in prostate MRI is often compromised by regional ambiguity. Automated segmentation methods relying on global overlap loss functions tend to be insensitive to surface deviations, leading to significant errors in ambiguous regions. Most existing uncertainty-aware methods treat uncertainty as a post-hoc quality indicator or prediction filter. We propose Contrastive Uncertainty Learning (CUL), a supervised framework that uses predictive uncertainty to directly guide representation learning. Using Monte Carlo Dropout, CUL identifies voxel-wise uncertainty and structures the embedding geometry accordingly: low-uncertainty, correctly predicted features form stable voxels while high-uncertainty or misclassified features are actively repelled, preventing feature collapse at difficult boundaries. A hard-case analysis reveals a $22{\times}$ asymmetry between significantly improved and degraded cases ($18.8\%$ vs $0.9\%$). Ablation studies show that removing uncertainty-guided sample selection eliminates improvements for the transition zone, while replacing the contrastive objective with a boundary loss additionally removes bladder improvements, demonstrating that both uncertainty-guided sampling and the contrastive objective are essential for the observed gains. These findings demonstrate that the proposed uncertainty-guided representation learning is a useful mechanism for reducing the risk of extreme errors in safety-critical medical segmentation

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) policies for planetary landing and other aerospace guidance, navigation, and control tasks are commonly trained under randomized disturbances to improve robustness. This work investigates adversarial reinforcement learning to discover challenging off-nominal conditions. A secondary agent generates physically constrained perturbations that degrade the performance of a landing controller, forming an asymmetric sequential zero-sum game. Training proceeds in three stages: baseline controller learning, robustness enhancement with stochastic perturbations, and an iterative adversarial phase in which the controller and disturbance generator are alternately optimized. Title: FUSE: Fairness-aware Update Screening against Byzantine Exploitation in $q$-Fair Federated Learning

  • Authors: Tiantong Sun, Jinlin Liu, Xingru Wei, Zequan Fang, Wei Chen, Qiang Yang, Xiaojin Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20264
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20264
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20264]] FUSE: Fairness-aware Update Screening against Byzantine Exploitation in $q$-Fair Federated Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20264)
  • Keywords: protect, attack, robust, federate, fair
  • Abstract: Client-level fair federated learning, in the form of $q$-fair federated learning ($q$-FFL), agnostic federated learning, or tilted empirical risk minimization, has emerged as an attractive solution to equalize utility across heterogeneous clients. This paradigm grants high-loss clients greater relative influence, while a central server aggregates their loss-weighted updates into a single shared model. Yet, the Byzantine robustness of $q$-fair federated learning has been largely neglected, leaving the server unable to tell legitimate fairness-induced amplification from malicious update scaling. To address this critical gap, we formalize the problem of Byzantine-robust $q$-fair federated learning under an \emph{update-only interface}, in which the server observes nothing but the fairness-scaled client updates. Our analysis under shared class-conditional label skew shows that fairness scaling preserves the nonnegative class-mixture structure of honest updates, while replacing their unit-sum constraint by a client-dependent radial scale. Moving beyond magnitude-based screening, we show that this warped geometry supplies two complementary tests, one radial and one structural, that a single norm cannot provide. We then introduce FUSE, a structure-aware robust aggregator that uses a small trusted labeled dataset for coefficient recovery, scale and cone-consistency screening, and post-screening step-size control. Across four benchmarks and seven Byzantine attacks, FUSE improves seed-wise worst-attack accuracy over the strongest baseline on each benchmark by 3.9--30.1 percentage points, while largely preserving the influence of the high-loss honest clients that fairness is meant to protect.

  • Abstract: Conditional generative models are increasingly used for scientific prediction beyond the direct training support. It remains unclear how these models learn interactions and when these successfully extrapolate to unseen conditions. Finally, we show that aggregate fidelity metrics can conceal whether models learn and extrapolate interactions.

In this paper, we rethink backdoor detection by shifting the diagnostic paradigm from static observations to dynamic output evolution. The attention branch measures how image-text attention allocation evolves during early generation, whereas the logits branch quantifies the sensitivity of the output distribution to modal perturbations through interventional ablations. We then convert these trajectories into compact sample-level metrics and normalize them against negative-control signatures so that natural perturbations are less likely to be mistaken for backdoor evidence. Extensive evaluations show that our approach substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines such as BYE and TCAP across diverse attack families.

  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20337
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20337
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20337]] Training Dynamics of Transformers on Unbounded Inputs: Attention Learning Can Lag Output Layer Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20337)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: The success of transformers is attributed to several factors, including their capacity to express formal languages over variable-length inputs, and ability of the attention mechanism to focus on relevant input features. However, there is limited understanding of the training dynamics of shallow transformers, specifically how to decouple the learning of the attention and the output (feedforward) layer. A natural extension of the theory of one-step gradient feature learning in feedforward networks suggests that one could first learn the attention weights with a single gradient step, and then train the output layer. We show that this fails for a shallow transformer trained to recognize a star-free language from unbounded-length input. We prove that the transformer can be trained to recognize the language with a three-phase gradient descent: the output layer is updated first, followed by an attention update, and the output layer is fine-tuned in the third phase. In contrast, the language cannot be learned by training only the attention or output layer, or by a single attention-layer update followed by finetuning the output layer. We empirically show that ``attention learning lagging output layer learning" is beneficial in other language recognition, regression, and in-context learning tasks as well.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models have made significant progress in referring expression comprehension (REC), yet most REC benchmarks still focus on final-target localization. Complex queries often involve intermediate visual references with different dependencies on prior grounding, but final-only evaluation leaves this process unexamined. To address this gap, we formulate trajectory-level REC, in which models receive an ordered sequence of grounding steps constructed from a single complex query and localize the target regions associated with each step. We instantiate this setting in ReferFlow-Bench, which characterizes structured region transitions through four trajectory types, namely Refinement, Shift, Reset, and Mixture. Experiments show that current models struggle to consistently ground intermediate visual references across successive steps. We further propose ReferFlow, which decouples interleaved text-latent trajectory generation from coordinate decoding. Specifically, Stateful Region Latent Reasoning represents each target region as an object-indexed state composed of continuous latents, with explicit visual and spatial supervision to preserve region information for subsequent grounding. Trajectory-Aware Parallel Decoding then recovers all coordinate blocks in parallel through four geometry-constrained refinement rounds, with each block conditioned on its corresponding trajectory prefix, thereby reducing within-box sequential dependencies and eliminating cross-box autoregression. We further apply Grounding-Aware GRPO to promote consistent grounding across steps and learn adaptive trajectory generation from complex REC queries. ReferFlow achieves leading performance on general REC benchmarks and ReferFlow-Bench.

Title: Do AI Agents Gossip? Evaluative Talk in Human and AI-Agent Online Communities

  • Authors: Yong-Hui Hua, Chih-Han Lin, Shu-Kai Hsieh
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20360
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20360
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20360]] Do AI Agents Gossip? Evaluative Talk in Human and AI-Agent Online Communities(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20360)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Gossip, defined as evaluative talk about absent third parties, is widely seen as a uniquely human social practice. This study examines whether comparable discourse emerges spontaneously in a community of autonomous AI agents. We compare Moltbook, a Reddit-style platform populated by AI agents, with PTT, a large human-operated bulletin board in Taiwan. To reduce differences attributable to communicative setting, we analyze two public, asynchronous forums using the same operational definition of gossip. Gossip occurs on Moltbook, but its prevalence is 2.6 to 6 times lower than on PTT, depending on the denominator used. On both platforms, gossip frequently targets locally prominent figures, tends to elicit agreement from the first respondent, and is rarely presented as unsupported rumor. The clearest difference concerns how subsequent responses develop. Among threads containing at least one codable reply, challenges occur in 65.1% of human threads but only 21.3% of agent threads. Moreover, following an early aligning response, consensus remains unchallenged in 83.6% of agent threads, compared with 44.7% of human threads. This thread-level contrast remains after controlling for thread depth and board composition and under recalibration against two human annotators. Estimates based on individual reply turns are less robust and are therefore reported as bounds. These findings suggest that AI-agent communities reproduce the informational dimension of gossip, while the extent to which gossip performs comparable relational functions remains unresolved.

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Neural partial differential equation (PDE) surrogates trained with mean squared error (MSE) can capture dominant dynamics but often lose high-frequency information and fine-scale structures. To address these limitations, we introduce Refine and Align(ReAl), a model-agnostic plug-in that augments pretrained PDE surrogates to improve the reconstruction of high-frequency structures associated with complex physical phenomena. In the alignment stage, to mitigate the generative discrepancies amplified in the autoregressive rollout, particularly in chaotic systems, we generate multiple trajectories from the same input and reinforce the refiner with trajectory-level rewards, thereby achieving long-horizon dynamical alignment. Experiments across diverse surrogate architectures and PDE tasks demonstrate that ReAl consistently improves high-frequency fidelity, forecasting accuracy, and long-horizon stability.

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: When does a human correction become useful learning, and what establishes better segmentation at matched human effort? It compares active learning, machine teaching, interactive segmentation, and sequential predictive evaluation using a common mathematical account of target information, predictive error, response predictability, and human work. Title: When Does Momentum Help in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards?

  • Authors: Kaifei Wang, Zikang Shan, Liwei Wang, Yinyu Ye, Han Zhong
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20413
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20413
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20413]] When Does Momentum Help in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20413)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Momentum is a classical tool for accelerating convex optimization and a standard component of modern large language model training. Yet recent empirical work has questioned the usefulness of first-moment momentum in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We study this question through a bandit formulation of RLVR with bias-corrected exponential moving average (EMA) momentum, where the EMA coefficient $\beta$ controls how quickly the weights on past gradients decay. When policy gradients are evaluated exactly, we prove that increasing $\beta$ within a range that prevents overshoot strictly accelerates optimization. When gradients are estimated from sampled responses, a positive expected gain can be obscured by sampling fluctuations. We show that as the learning rate $\eta$ decreases, the expected gain with fixed $\beta$ vanishes faster than these fluctuations, whereas increasing $\beta$ to keep $\eta/(1-\beta)$ within the safe regime preserves a nonvanishing gain that eventually dominates them. Bandit simulations quantitatively validate these predictions. RLVR experiments on Qwen3-4B-Base using SGD with EMA and AdamW support our principle of choosing larger coefficients in practical LLM post-training. On Qwen2.5-32B with AdamW, simply increasing $\beta_1$ improves mean validation accuracy by nearly $2$ percentage points over the conventional choice, demonstrating the principle's effectiveness at a larger model scale.

Title: One Size Does Not Fit All: Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM

  • Authors: Alessia Rossi, Felipe Campelo, Gabriel L. Oliveira
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20415
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20415
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20415]] One Size Does Not Fit All: Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20415)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Poor generalisation in deep neural networks is frequently associated with convergence to sharp minima. Sharpness-Aware Minimisation (SAM) improves generalisation of deep neural networks by minimising a combination of the training loss and the local sharpness of the loss landscape, biasing convergence towards flat minima. However, SAM relies on a fixed perturbation radius $\rho$ that ignores local curvature, leading to inefficient behaviour: the perturbation may be too small to escape poor basins defined by sharp local minima, or too large to allow convergence to solutions with good loss-sharpness tradeoff. We reinterpret $\rho$ as the intensity of the perturbation signal and propose Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM (CAP-SAM), a geometry-aware method that modulates the perturbation radius based on a sharpness ratio, enabling a more effective search for robust minima on the loss landscape. We prove that CAP-SAM preserves the same convergence rate as standard SAM in non-convex stochastic settings. Extensive experiments indicate that our approach consistently outperforms SAM and its most prominent variants across diverse network architectures on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet.

Title: Phantomware: a Data Augmentation Framework for the Detection of Emerging Malware Campaigns

  • Authors: Antonino Rullo
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20427
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20427
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20427]] Phantomware: a Data Augmentation Framework for the Detection of Emerging Malware Campaigns(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20427)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Detecting unseen malware variants remains a major challenge as malware families continuously evolve. This issue is exacerbated in data-scarce scenarios such as emerging malware campaigns, where only a few samples are available to train robust detectors. A common strategy is to proactively generate malware variants and use them as augmentation data to expose models to a broader range of potential threats. Existing approaches that generate functional malware variants through adversarial manipulation suffer from several important limitations. Generated samples must remain executable and preserve their malicious behavior, requiring costly validation through execution and analysis. Moreover, the adversarial objective provides limited control over the resulting augmentation dataset: it cannot guarantee the generation of sufficiently diverse variants, or even a single successful sample, making the effectiveness of the augmentation process inherently unpredictable. Finally, these approaches typically rely on a well-trained detector to guide generation, an assumption that may not hold in data-scarce scenarios such as early-stage malware campaigns. We formally and empirically demonstrate these limitations and propose Phantomware, a data augmentation framework designed for extreme data scarce scenarios. Phantomware operates entirely in the representation space of a pretrained malware detector, avoiding the costly generation and validation of functional binaries. By exploiting latent-space geometry, it generates synthetic malware representations of a target family in a controlled manner, providing a constructive guarantee on the generation of diverse training samples. Extensive experiments on real malware families demonstrate that Phantomware improves detection of unseen variants and provides robustness against both real and adversarially generated malware samples.

Title: The COUNT: Counting N-grams Unlocks Scaling on Host Memory and Training-Free Adaptation

  • Authors: Ian Magnusson, Noah A. Smith, Pang Wei Koh
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20456
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20456

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: The scaling relationship of compute and performance for language models (LMs) has been extensively studied; more recently, models are being designed to scale memory use, as well. In particular, adding embeddings for n-grams scales memory separately from compute use, because each pass only processes individual embedding rows, which are much smaller than the storage used for the whole embedding table. However, existing methods train embeddings from scratch, which needs expensive GPU memory for fast reading and writing. Meanwhile, the ordinary memory of the host system is cheap and underutilized by these training methods. To scale on host memory, we introduce COUNT (Counting Offline Unlocks N-gram Transformers), which builds n-gram embeddings without adding learned parameters by storing precomputed n-gram counts on host. We use the corpus frequencies to weight together the LM's own unembeddings of the next tokens most often following the current n-gram history. At a fixed compute budget, COUNT can be added as an additional input embedding for pretraining 1B-parameter LMs to improve validation loss as much as an LM with 57% more compute. If GPU memory is limited, using COUNT to let backbone parameters fill GPU memory gives better performance than splitting memory with additional learned n-gram embeddings. Beyond efficiency, COUNT allows switching in a different table of n-gram counts from a domain-specific corpus at inference time to adapt to a new task without any training.

To the best of our knowledge, OLiVES is the first outdoor low-light video dataset captured using professional cinema-grade cameras. It provides over 25,000 aligned normal- and low-light frame pairs, along with over 46,000 VOS annotations. We benchmark state-of-the-art LLVE and VOS methods on OLiVES and show that training on it improves performance on both tasks. The OLiVES project page is available at: this https URL.

  • Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions.   • Abstract: Generative super-resolution (SR) can recover sharp detail in computed tomography (CT) images from severely downsampled observations, but the missing high-frequency content is not uniquely determined by the low-resolution input. Title: Bayesian Learning to Select Fair Classifiers

  • Authors: Saatvik Kher, Hyungrok Do, Judy Zhong, Padhraic Smyth
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20514
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20514

  • Keywords: fair, large language model
  • Abstract: Evaluations of classifiers (AI or human or agents) often assume a batch setting in which every agent produces labels on every example in a test set, which can be expensive or impractical in many contexts. This motivates online policies that adaptively select agents to query as examples arrive and update policies as outcomes are observed. We study the problem of selecting, in an online stream, the agent that best balances (i) predictive performance and (ii) subgroup fairness of a sensitive attribute, and that allows for delayed observations of true class labels. We formulate this as a Bayesian multi-armed bandit problem using Thompson Sampling, explicitly balancing accuracy and fairness. We conduct empirical evaluations on real-world datasets spanning problems involving tabular classifiers, large language models, and human labelers, demonstrating substantial reductions in regret relative to baselines. Our theoretical contributions include guarantees on the performance of the selected agent that incorporate both the accuracy and fairness aspects of the problem.

Title: Tulva: Practical Asynchronous Threshold FHE

  • Authors: Valerio Cini, Antonio Guimarães, Russell W. F. Lai, Michał Osadnik, Ivy K. Y. Woo

  • Abstract: We present Tulva, a practical threshold FHE scheme which equips a base FHE scheme with an asynchronous threshold decryption mechanism in a modular way. Title: One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness

  • Authors: Artin Tajdini, Manpreet Kaur, Peng Qi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20576
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20576]] One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.

Title: Continuum Attention

  • Authors: Afshin Khadangi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20586
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20586
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20586]] Continuum Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20586)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We introduce Continuum Attention (CA), a causal Transformer attention operator that replaces discrete key and value lookup with integration over a continuous, piecewise linear memory curve. Each query induces an exponential relevance density along the curve. Integrating the density and interpolated values yields exact normalized attention from closed form exponential moments, including half token boundary contributions. The gradients follow analytically from moments of order at most two, enabling parallel GPU implementations while retaining quadratic sequence complexity and the standard Transformer decoder. Compared with ordinary softmax and focal attention, CA reduces mean validation negative log likelihood by 0.0200 and 0.0480 nats per byte, respectively, winning all paired comparisons. An additional disjoint validation slice yields a 0.0457 nats per byte advantage over the strongest selected softmax baseline, with a paired 95% confidence interval. These findings motivate continuous integration as a promising attention alternative while leaving larger scale generalization open.

Title: GeoThinker-$F$: Endogenous Predictive World Representations for Fast Spatial Intelligence

  • Authors: Haoyuan Li, Qihang Cao, Huanyuan Zhou, Xin Hu, Sijia Chen, Zihan Guo, Kun Xiang, Yunshuang Nie, Tao Tang, Xinlong Zheng, Jia-Wang Bian, Xianming Liu, Xiaodan Liang

We also release CommerceGen-Bench, a benchmark of hard cases collected from live traffic across all product categories. On it, our framework achieves the highest overall scores among the evaluated open-source baselines on both editing tracks, with and without prompt enhancement. It also matches much larger closed-source models, and beats several of them at Instruction Following and Product Integration - the two properties that determine whether an image can be published.

Title: Representation Conditioning for Generation

  • Authors: Saeed Razavi, Xingjian Leng, Liang Zheng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20608
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20608
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20608]] Representation Conditioning for Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20608)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Representation-alignment methods effectively accelerate the diffusion transformer training. REPA, for example, uses a pretrained vision encoder to supervise an intermediate layer through an MLP projection head which is discarded after training. It aims to import semantics from the vision encoder to the diffusion backbone. While this objective is achieved at early layers by back-propagating the alignment loss, there is no guarantee that later layers, supervised by the velocity prediction loss alone, can well access such semantics. We propose a simple solution, representation conditioning (RECON), to improve semantics in later layers. During training, we feed the pretrained vision encoder tokens into later layers; during inference, because we no longer have the clean image, we reuse the MLP head (as a proxy to the vision encoder) and project its output tokens to the later layers. Our method adds no parameters and leads to much faster convergence: RECON is $63\times$ faster than SiT and $25\times$ faster than REPA. Code is available at this https URL

Title: Can Learning to Answer Spatial Questions Improve Novel-View Generation?

  • Authors: Jahyeok Koo, Hyeonseo Yu, Seungho Jang, Jaewoo Jung, Jiwon Kang, Seokju Cho, Seungryong Kim
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20610
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.20610
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.20610]] Can Learning to Answer Spatial Questions Improve Novel-View Generation?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20610)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Generating novel views from limited observations requires preserving both the appearance and spatial structure of a scene. Although recent approaches exploit fine-grained geometric cues, they do not explicitly model high-level semantic and spatial properties such as object identity, attributes, and inter-object relationships. In this work, we investigate whether 3D language understanding can provide complementary supervision for novel-view generation. We introduce Ask4NV, built on a pretrained unified multimodal model with a Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) architecture. Our model incorporates camera geometry into both VAE and ViT representations and leverages understanding features during generation through joint self-attention with a modality-aware attention masking strategy. Experiments demonstrate strong image quality and geometric consistency in novel-view generation, alongside competitive 3D language understanding. Further analysis shows that joint training improves cross-view correspondence in VAE representations and similarity in a 3D-aware feature space.
